theory-2: why residual leaves, the gradient trigger and the consistent targets gave nothing in play, the DAG re-examined,
and what to try next (2026-11-10, evening; resident math analyst)

Read-only outside this folder. Read: theory/summary.txt (the previous analysis), RESULTS.md sections 1, 3-6, 8, 10,
PATH-FORWARD.md, trigger/summary.txt, target/summary.txt, resid/resid4.patch (the residual-leaf code), the five
experiments' config.json, target-src/src/{delegation,probit,logreg}.rs. Simulations and measurements here:
  sim_search_noise.py/.out   uneven evaluator noise under depth-1 and depth-2 search (pure Python);
  sim_residual.py/.out       residual leaves as implemented, as theorised (joint), and copies, under drift;
  sim_cal_routing.py/.out    what a consistent target routes on when leaves differ in calibration only (exact);
  who_wins.out               U (routing explained by who's winning) for single-split's T-L-Le and T-L-Nc at v999;
  routing/                   routing shares of the probit error trees; leaf extraction; drift_corr.py/.out: how much
                             of a copy tree's leaf drift is common to its leaves (existing models, two experiments).
CPU: about 11 CPU-minutes in all (sims ~7, measurements ~4), nice 19, one or two processes at a time, python3 -I, no numpy.

Labels: THEOREM (with argument), COUNTEREXAMPLE, CONJECTURE, SIMULATION (toy), EVIDENCE (our data). Terms are defined
where first used. "Flat" is the one-leaf model (F-L, F-P). "Leaf" is a prediction model at the bottom of a tree,
"router" the classifier that picks a child. Elo numbers carry no plus sign. The user is referred to as "the user".


=====================================================================================================================
0. DIGEST
=====================================================================================================================

The three designs were right about what they fixed and silent about what decides play. None of the three results is a
counterexample to the previous theory; each is a case of the theory's prediction being either below the noise floor,
or beside the quantity that decides play, or (for the consistent targets) a fix of one wrong assumption that left a
second one standing.

MAIN EXPLANATIONS
1. Residual (additive) leaves. The code does what the theory asked in the way that matters: the shared part tracks
   common drift B times faster, and SIMULATION here shows the implemented variant (shadow trained on its own loss,
   delta on the sum's loss, sums inherited) tracks drift as well as the jointly trained model (loss 0.63 against the
   copies' 0.66 one thousand examples after a common shift). Two things the theory did not have: EVIDENCE that the
   league's drift is only about half common (leaf-pair weight-change correlation 0.2-0.7, common share 0.47-0.50 of
   the squared change in the 9-leaf copy tree; 0.01-0.22 across the three phase leaves of single-split), and
   SIMULATION that the residual leaf is 20-40% NOISIER than a copy (the sum of two independently stepped models). The
   run also used no shrinkage (--residual-l2 0), so the one property that would reduce noise and help a search was
   off. Kind: (ii) half, (i) half (the "common drift" conjecture holds only within a phase), plus (iii).
2. Gradient trigger T4. It decides WHEN to split, not WHERE: the installed router is a fresh cluster router, as in the
   control arm, so both arms grow the same kind of tree. EVIDENCE from its own log: the first-order gain it measures
   is almost the same at every leaf (T/n 0.0022-0.0038 nats per example over 36 tests), so at Z 9.5 it fired where the
   standard error was smallest, which is a sample-size rule like the stall trigger. Both arms made 3-4 splits in 400
   cycles; their trees differ by the timing of one or two splits, which is worth one split's transient (30-90 Elo for
   40-100 cycles, so 10-20 averaged over the run), under the noise floor. Kind: (iii) and (i): a first-order gain is
   necessary, not sufficient, and it ignores the variance cost of a split.
3. Consistent targets T1. They fixed the target's inconsistency (THEOREM 1.2-1.5 of theory) and kept its deeper flaw:
   theory 1.4 already said every error router is "an evaluator in disguise". NEW THEOREM (section 1.3): if two leaves
   differ only by calibration (temperature) the consistent target is a function of the position's CERTAINTY |z|; if
   they differ by lean, of its SIGN. Both are label-proxy partitions, both are self-consistent fixed points of routed
   training for every target, both lie inside a 3-way LR router's class (material is linear in the squares), and their
   gradient signal is about ten times the competence signal (a base-rate difference of 0.3-0.5 against a calibration
   error of 0.03). EVIDENCE: single-split's T-L-Le at v999 has U 0.37 (37% of its routing entropy is explained by who
   is winning) against 0.03 for the cluster tree; the error trees' ply-2 deficit is monotone in U (Le -111, Te -37,
   Ne -16); gap targets lowered U to 0.07-0.11, not to 0.03. Probit leaves make it worse because their posterior
   shrinkage gives data-rich and data-poor leaves different temperatures, exactly the certainty attractor; it is
   target-independent, as observed (all four T-P-Le variants 119-142 below F-P at ply 2 and 36-84 at ply 1). Kind: (i).
4. DAG router splits. Predicted null (theory 5.2). THEOREM (section 2): a shared-leaf DAG is a tree with equality
   constraints on its leaves, so its only gain is variance, at most (L - L') d / (2 n_eff) nats, about 0.001 at our
   sizes; a home DAG is a deeper gate with the routing headroom (0.001-0.003) as ceiling; path cells add a sum of step
   functions with 3 numbers each. Nothing in the five studies could exceed 0.003 nats. Kind: (i) and (iv).
5. Cross-cutting. (a) 0.003 nats is the whole budget of every routing effect ever measured here, and the same 0.004
   offline gain gave 70-100, 10-40 and -25 to 26 Elo in three runs of one recipe: the Elo per nat is not a constant,
   and every experiment whose expected effect was 0.003 nats or less was underpowered by design (version noise 17 SD,
   window drift 30, run-to-run 50-90). (b) The "ply-2 dip" of LR trees is mostly measurement: the ply-2 scale is 2x
   the ply-1 scale and so is its noise; over 45 lines the systematic residual is about 25 Elo (ply-2 scale) with a
   standard error near 12. PATH-FORWARD's mechanism (b), uneven leaf noise exploited by the min over replies, is a
   COUNTEREXAMPLE in simulation: with independent noise the min PENALISES noisy regions, which is benign (uneven
   noise costs more at depth 1 than at depth 2). What the simulation does say is that shrinking leaves towards a shared
   base cuts search regret by 40-50% at both depths.

TOP THREE SUGGESTIONS (section 3 ranks eight)
S1. Shrinkage leaves: the residual leaf with a real L2 pull of the delta to zero (the --residual-l2 option exists),
    which makes the tree a hierarchical model (shared base plus small per-region corrections). Prediction: the post-
    split cost (70-150 Elo for 40-120 cycles) shrinks because a young leaf predicts as its parent until its data say
    otherwise, and the evaluator's noise falls. Test: offline on an existing stream at three values of l2 (hours),
    then one ply-1 line against the copy tree.
S2. Calibration-invariant error targets: compute the children's losses after a per-child affine recalibration of
    their log-odds (two numbers per child per class, fitted online). THEOREM: this makes the target invariant to per-
    child temperature and lean, so the two label-proxy attractors vanish and only ranking (competence) differences
    remain. Prediction: U falls to cluster level (0.03), no child starves, and probit leaves under LR routers stop
    collapsing. Test: offline in bddstudy's setup (U, effective leaves), then a ply-1 line of T-P-Le.
S3. Growth as boosting: use T4's per-region gradient directions a_k to INITIALISE the children (theta + eps a_k) and
    install the probe as the router (--split-gradient-install exists). THEOREM: this is the steepest first-order
    descent direction of the split; it also breaks symmetry with the data instead of a hash. Prediction: the split's
    transient halves. Test: offline in trigger's setup (install and init on, off), then ply 1.
And a rule: no experiment whose expected gain is under 0.005 nats at ply 2 without two seeds and a ply-1 twin.


=====================================================================================================================
1. EXPLANATIONS
=====================================================================================================================

Notation as in theory/summary.txt. Four kinds of answer: (i) the theory or an assumption fails; (ii) the code does
not do what the theory assumed; (iii) the measurement cannot show it; (iv) the gain is real but too small.

---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.1 Residual (additive) leaves: residual-leaves (T3), copy tree 73 at ply 1 and 6 at ply 2 against flat; residual
    tree 38 behind the copy tree at the end, ahead only in cycles 51-150
---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

What was built (code, resid4.patch; the run used --leaf-residual --residual-inherit-sums, l2 0, delta rate 1):
- A delegator keeps a SHADOW, a flat LR trained on every example that passes the delegator, on ITS OWN loss
  (train_shadow: revision.shadow.train(data, label)).
- Each child leaf is shadow + delta; the delta takes an AdaGrad step on the SUM's loss with the shadow held fixed
  (train_leaf_below), on the examples routed to it. With inherit-sums the delta starts at zero WITH the shadow's AdaGrad
  sums, so at the moment of the split the children are exactly the copies a plain split makes.
- A residual leaf that splits materialises shadow + delta as the new shadow.

(ii) The theory's model (5.4) had every node on the path take the SAME gradient, that of the sum's loss. The code trains
the shadow on its own loss. THEOREM (same optimum, different path): with routing fixed, the shadow converges to the
flat optimum theta* on its stream and the delta to argmin of region k's loss at theta* + delta, so the sum converges to
region k's optimum, the same point as a copy; the two designs differ only in dynamics (the joint model descends one
convex objective; the implemented one is a two-stage fit with a moving base). SIMULATION (sim_residual.py, binary LR,
10 features, 2 regions, 8 seeds): the implemented variant tracks a COMMON drift as fast as the joint model and much
faster than copies (region-0 loss 1,000 examples after the shift: flat 0.675, copies 0.664, joint 0.632, implemented
0.631; 3,000 after: 0.631, 0.614, 0.583, 0.580), and is level with copies under a REGION drift. So the deviation from
the theory does not cost tracking. What it costs is NOISE: the prediction of a residual leaf is the sum of two
independently stepped models, and its across-seed SD at fixed probe positions is 30-50% above a copy's (at t = 30k,
stationary: copies 0.066/0.070 by region, joint 0.086/0.088, implemented 0.091/0.095; at t = 50k: copies 0.058/0.042,
implemented 0.083/0.063). What a search does with noise is in 1.5.

(i) The conjecture the theory flagged: that self-play drift is "common enough". EVIDENCE (drift_corr.py; leaves of the
league's own models extracted with `chessai leaves`, weights compared between versions over the weights both leaves
trained on):
- residual-leaves' copy tree (9 leaves) over cycles 250-325 and 325-399: leaf-pair correlations of weight change 0.19
  to 0.68 (median about 0.45); each leaf's change correlates 0.19-0.73 with flat F-L's change over the same versions;
  the mean change explains 0.498 and 0.465 of the leaves' squared change, against 1/9 = 0.11 if changes were
  independent. About half the drift is common.
- single-split's T-L-Nc-merge (3 phase leaves) over cycles 800-900 and 900-999: pairwise 0.01-0.22; the mean explains
  0.437 and 0.398 against a baseline of 1/3. Across phases the drift is mostly region-specific; the opening and
  endgame leaves (1 and 3) follow flat (0.61-0.78) and the middlegame leaf (2) does not (0.13-0.23).
So the shared base in a residual tree tracks about half the drift B times faster, and the sibling leaves that share
most (near-copies within one phase) are also the ones with the least to gain from being separate.

(iii) The copy tree itself was level with flat at ply 2 from cycle 200 and 73 ahead at ply 1; the residual tree is
compared with that. A 38-Elo end-of-run gap at ply 2 is one window of drift (30) plus version noise (17). The residual
tree's early lead (27-29 in cycles 51-150, the two windows after the first splits) is what the theory predicts for the
transient, and its later deficit is what the noise argument predicts once the leaves have filled. Neither is replicated.

(iv) In the toy the gain is 0.03-0.05 nats on an 11-weight model with 60k examples, where the variance term d/(2n) is
large. In chess d is about 2,700 per leaf and a line sees about 171k positions a cycle (561k games over 400 cycles,
122 plies each), 68M in all, so the leaves' variance cost of (B - 1) d / (2 n) is about 0.0001 nats by cycle 100: the
copies' "slow filling" after a split is not the classical variance term. CONJECTURE: under AdaGrad on a drifting
stream the effective sample of a leaf is the number of its examples within the drift's time-scale, not its total;
that is what the shared base shortens (a copy's step per unit time is 1/B of the base's in the saturated regime,
theory 5.4 iv).

Cheap test that tells (ii)/(iii) from (iv): the two trees' leaf NOISE, not their loss: the per-position SD of the
predicted log-odds across 5 adjacent versions on a fixed set of positions, per leaf, residual against copy. The theory
here predicts residual 20-40% noisier at equal loss; if it is not, the sum's noise is not the issue and (iii) stands.

---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.2 Gradient trigger T4: trigger-gradient, T4 and the stall trigger at limit 8: 48 and 27 at ply 1, 3 and -8 at ply 2
---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

(ii) Two gaps between the theory (6.3) and the build, both documented in trigger/summary.txt:
- The theory's split criterion is "the norm of the leaf's mean gradient over a candidate region", the region to be
  chosen to maximise it. The build tests ONE candidate partition per leaf, the one a label-free NB cluster probe makes,
  and (without --split-gradient-install) INSTALLS a different, fresh cluster router with a hash start. So T4 tests a
  phase-like partition and installs a phase-like partition: the same tree kind as the stall trigger's, split at a
  different moment.
- The statistic is first-order. A split pays (1/2) sum_k n_k a_k' H^-1 a_k minus the variance cost; T4 measures the
  numerator's first-order part and nothing of the cost. It cannot stop a split that gains 0.001 and costs 0.003.

EVIDENCE (the T4 line's training log, 36 tests): T/n, the first-order gain per example along the probe's partition, is
0.0022 at the median and 0.0038 at most, nearly the same at every leaf and every window; z = T/se ranged 2.4-11.6 with
median 5.2, and the three splits that fired (z 10.2, 11.6, 10.7) were tests with small se (76-233; the non-firing
tests I listed had 260-755), i.e. more or more consistent games, not a larger gain per example. So at the league's 1M windows T4 reduced to a sample-size rule.
The trigger summary's "known limitation" (unfinished main effects show through shared weights) is probably why every
leaf disagrees at z 3-6.

(iii) Both arms grew 3-4 delegators in 400 cycles at limit 8. Their trees differ by the timing of one or two splits.
One split's transient is 70-150 Elo for 40-120 cycles (RESULTS section 3, "one leaf split costs a lot"), so moving
one split by 50 cycles changes a 400-cycle mean by 10-20 Elo, which is one version's noise. The offline advantage
(0.0045 nats over a same-leaves tree at 100k windows on a fixed dataset) came from AVOIDING splits the stall trigger
made on a fixed set of 60 rounds; in the league both triggers made few splits.

(iv) The first-order gain per example of 0.002-0.004 along a phase-like partition is, in the quadratic approximation
with curvature p(1-p) ~ 0.2, a full-fit gain of order 0.005 nats per example: the same size as the whole measured
routing headroom. The trigger was asked to tell splits apart inside a range that is below what play can resolve.

Cheap test: T4's z against the realised held-out gain of each split it fired, on the existing trigger-gradient models
(before/after each split, chessai eval-loss on one held-out round): if the gains are 0.002-0.005 for all three, (iv).

---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.3 Consistent targets T1: error-targets (LR) and error-targets-probit
---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

What the theory fixed, and what it left. Theory 1.2-1.6 proved Best, Soft and Balanced inconsistent and built targets
whose Bayes response is argmin_k L_k(x). It also proved (1.4) that the target of ANY error router is a functional of
p(y|x), and said routing adds skill only when children differ by region of competence; its remedy for that was
explicit label-free symmetry breaking (the hash bootstrap). The experiments show the remedy was not enough. Here is why.

THEOREM (calibration-only differences route by certainty; sim_cal_routing.py, exact). Let two leaves share the latent
score z(x) (the same ranking of positions) and differ only in temperature, q_k(W|x) = sigmoid(tau_k z), truth
sigmoid(z). Then E_y[l_A - l_B] depends on x only through |z|. If tau_A > 1 > tau_B (one over-, one under-confident),
the consistent target picks B below a threshold |z*| and A above it (tau 1.4/0.7: z* = 2.9, i.e. p(W) = 0.95); if
both are on the same side of 1, it picks the one nearer 1 everywhere and the other starves. Best's target depends on
sign(z) as well (A is "best" on W outcomes when hotter). Neither partition is a region of competence: the leaves rank
positions identically. A B = 3 LR router represents the certainty partition exactly (logits -a z, 0, a z with
intercepts) and z is linear in the squares (material), so the attractor is inside the router's class.

THEOREM (the label-proxy attractors are fixed points of routed training, for every target). Suppose the router sends
positions with p(W|x) > 1/2 to A and the rest to B (a lean partition). Each leaf, refit on its region, is calibrated to
its region's base rate, so at every x near the boundary the leaf whose base rate matches p(W|x) has the lower expected
loss: the consistent target reproduces the partition. The same holds for a certainty partition when the leaves'
confidences differ (above). So lean and certainty partitions are self-consistent under Best, Soft, Balanced AND the
consistent targets; consistency changes which fixed points are reachable, not whether these exist (theory 3.2 proved
the lean one for Best).

CONJECTURE (why the dynamics prefer them, with sizes). For two leaves differing by a small perturbation delta, the loss
gap at (x, y) is (q - y) delta.phi(x) + O(delta^2). Its label-free (competence) part is (q - p) delta.phi, of the size
of the leaf's local calibration error, about 0.03; the lean attractor's drive is the base-rate difference between the
regions the router can make, 0.3-0.5. The second is ten times the first, so from any small perturbation the router's
first steps move along the outcome direction, the leaves' leans follow (theory 3.2), and the competence partition is
never reached. This is also why "gap" lowered U but did not remove it.

EVIDENCE.
- who_wins.out (this folder; the tool of fs/analysis-2026-11-10/leafoutcome): single-split at v999, T-L-Le U 0.37
  (I(leaf; who's winning decile) 0.235 of H(leaf) 0.634), T-L-Nc-merge U 0.03. The same experiment's ply-2 standing
  at 901-1000: T-L-Le -111, T-L-Te -37, T-L-Ne -16, T-L-Nc-merge 88, T-L-Tc-merge 99: monotone in how much the router
  routes by the label (U for NB and TAN error routers 0.02-0.12 in RESULTS section 5).
- target/summary.txt: gap targets cut U from 0.14-0.16 to 0.07-0.11, and leaf disagreement ROSE (|log-odds difference|
  1.3-2.0 against 0.7 under Best): the leaves specialise harder under the consistent target, along whatever the router
  found.
- error-targets at ply 1 (RESULTS section 1): the gap trees were 27-47 AHEAD of F-L, and -18 to -31 at ply 2. The gap
  targets did make a better ply-1 evaluator; the ply-2 verdict is the same measurement issue as 1.5.
- Probit leaves with LR error routers: 119-142 below F-P at 901-1000 at ply 2 and 36-84 at ply 1 for all four variants
  (Best, route-before-train, gap, gap with bootstrap); gap is WORSE than Best at ply 1 (-68 to -77 against -36 to
  -44). Target-independence is what the certainty-attractor theorem predicts for leaves whose temperatures differ by
  data count (a probit's predictive is Phi(mu / sqrt(1 + s^2)): the data-poor leaf is cooler). Shares at v999
  (routing/): Best 8/60/32, gap with bootstrap 34/24/42; single-split-probit's T-P-Le 23/28/49; T-P-Ne 54/46/0.4 (a
  2-way phase split, which works: 40-48 above F-P). The routing tool's recalibrated losses overflow on probit trees
  (known), so I could not measure U for them; the test below does it another way.

(ii) Checked: the build matches the theory (one weighted step on w/sum(w) with weight sum(w); silent on ties; the
bootstrap routes the first N by hash; route-before-train). No deviation found. One note: the bootstrap's hash third
gives each child an independent sampling perturbation, which is exactly the "small perturbation" the conjecture
starts from.

Kinds: (i) the assumption that consistency plus label-free symmetry breaking would reach competence regions; (iii) at
ply 1 the LR gap trees did beat flat; (iv) the competence signal is of the size of the headroom, 0.001-0.003 nats.

Cheap test that separates the certainty attractor from everything else, on existing models: for each T-P-Le leaf
(extracted with `chessai leaves`; routing/leaves-etp-best/ has them), the calibration slope fitted on the SAME held-out
set, and the mean |log-odds| of the flat F-P on the positions each leaf receives. The theorem predicts: slopes on
opposite sides of 1 across leaves, and the leaves' regions ordered by |log-odds| (one leaf gets the decided positions).
Needs the calibration command on probit leaves with a labelled round of decisive games (heldout-t01 is mostly draws).

---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.4 DAG router splits: five studies, seven variants, never above a same-leaves tree offline
---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

(i) Predicted. Theory 5.2: a home DAG is a tree over the same leaves with a deeper gate, bounded by the routing headroom
(0.001-0.003 nats as measured by re-pointing regions to their best leaf). Section 2 below gives the general ceiling for
every DAG variant. All seven variants are inside it, and the studies' confidence intervals (about +-0.0015) are the
size of the ceiling: nothing could have been seen. Kind (i) and (iv).

(ii) One deviation worth keeping in mind: the trigger fired router splits "on a climb that happens anyway" (bddstudy),
so most router splits were not where a router's class was too small. That is the same first-order/sample-size issue as
1.2, one level up.

---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.5 The ply-2 question: LR trees ahead at ply 1 and level at ply 2
---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

The facts (RESULTS section 1): over 1,328 comparisons ply-1 gaps are half the ply-2 gaps (slope 0.50, correlation
0.81); over the 45 tree lines in the last quarter the mean tree-minus-flat is 6.9 at ply 1 and -11.2 at ply 2; probit
trees agree across plies (the coordinator's ply-1 numbers for split-growth-probit: the limit-1 trees are 0 +- 6 at ply
1 and 20-51 at ply 2; limit 4 is 26-47 behind at ply 1 and 39-62 behind at ply 2).

(iii) THEOREM (scale and noise). If ply-1 Elo is a 0.5x compression of the same strength scale, a ply-1 gap of g has
the same signal-to-noise as a ply-2 gap of 2g only if ply-1 noise is also halved. Match noise in Elo units IS halved
(the Elo scale is what compresses), but version noise is a property of the models, measured at ply 2 (17 SD per
version, 30 per window). So per-window ply-2 contrasts of 20-50 are inside 1-2 SD; ply-1 contrasts of 25-45 (the LR
trees') are 2-3 SD on a compressed scale. The ply-1 verdicts look "consistent across runs" partly for this reason.
EVIDENCE of a residual: 45 lines, expected ply-2 mean 2 x 6.9 = 14, observed -11: a deficit of about 25 (ply-2 scale)
with a standard error near 12 if the ten runs are the independent units. Real but modest, and not replicated per
recipe (single-split's cluster trees were 81-99 ahead at ply 2).

COUNTEREXAMPLE to PATH-FORWARD's mechanism (b) (sim_search_noise.py; 30 moves, 30 replies, three regions with shares
0.6/0.25/0.15, true values N(0,1), reply spread 1, independent evaluator noise sigma_r per region, regret of the chosen
move in logits):
  setting A (no flat bias; tree noise 0.2/0.4/0.7, even tree 0.37 everywhere): depth 1 regret even 0.324, uneven
  0.353; depth 2 even 0.080, uneven 0.073. Uneven noise HURTS at depth 1 (winner's curse favours noisy moves) and
  helps slightly at depth 2 (the min over replies is a lower confidence bound: it penalises noisy regions, which is
  benign). Adding +2 sigma_r to compensate the min's bias ("tree-comp") makes depth 2 much WORSE (0.131), as it
  restores the winner's curse. The same sign in settings B-D; only with a very small reply spread (E, s_u 0.5) does
  unevenness cost at depth 2 (0.077 against 0.064).
So uneven, independent leaf noise does not produce a ply-2-specific penalty; if anything it predicts the opposite. What
does help at both depths in the toy is shrinkage to a shared base ("tree-shrunk", precision-weighted mean of the flat
and the leaf): regret 0.030-0.047 at depth 2 against 0.06-0.08 for every other evaluator. Caveat: in the toy the flat
and the leaf have independent errors; in chess they share data, so the gain is smaller.

Open and real: flat probit against flat LR changes the ply gain by 100-170 in matched settings (section 6). Not
addressed here; the test is to score both on the positions a ply-2 search actually visits (a saved tournament's search
nodes) rather than on game positions, since that is where they differ.

---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.6 Size: the break-even and the Elo per nat
---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Theory 3.5's break-even n* = (B - 1) d / (2 g) with d 2,700, B 3, g 0.003 is 0.9M positions per leaf; a local line
sees about 171k positions a cycle (1.1), so a 3-leaf tree passes n* by cycle 16 and a 9-leaf tree by cycle 50. Yet the
observed recovery takes 40-120 cycles. So the classical variance term does not set the cost of a split; the drift
time-scale does (1.1, iv). On the gain side: g = 0.001-0.003 nats is the WHOLE of every routing effect measured
(headroom by re-pointing, NB against LR routers 0.0017, sharing 0.005, the best structure on offline-c15 0.004). The
Elo per nat is not stable: the same 0.004-nat recipe (T-L-Nc-merge, one split) gave 70-100, 10-40 and -25 to 26 Elo at
ply 2 in three runs. THEOREM (trivial but binding): an experiment that contrasts two arms whose expected offline
difference is at most 0.003 nats, with per-window noise of 30 Elo and run-to-run spread of 50-90, cannot be decided by
one run at any length. Every one of the three experiments was of this kind (T3: residual against copy, same leaves;
T4: same tree kind, different timing; T1: same tree, different target under a shared attractor).


=====================================================================================================================
2. THE DAG DESIGN, RE-EXAMINED
=====================================================================================================================

2.1 THEOREM (what a DAG with shared leaves computes). Let a tree have L leaves and a DAG the same routers with L' < L
distinct leaves (some paths share a leaf). The DAG's function class is the tree's with equality constraints among
leaves, so its population optimum is no better. With finite data each shared leaf trains on the union of its paths'
streams, so the DAG's excess loss is about L' d / (2 n_eff) against the tree's L d / (2 n_eff): the gain is at most
(L - L') d / (2 n_eff). With d 2,700, n_eff 10M (a drift window, not the total) and L - L' = 5, that is 0.0007 nats.
Sharing pays only when d grows (richer leaves) or n_eff shrinks (fast drift) or many regions want the same function.

2.2 THEOREM (home DAG = deeper gate; theory 5.2 restated). Router splits with home targets grow the gate, not the
experts. Ceiling: the routing headroom on the existing leaves, measured 0.001-0.003. A deeper gate over the same three
leaves cannot gain what re-pointing every position to its best leaf cannot.

2.3 THEOREM (path cells = a tiny hidden layer). With intercept-only cells, a position's logit is theta_leaf.phi(x) +
sum over the sibling sets on its path of b_{set, value}. Each set's value is a hard decision of a linear (NB or LR)
router, so the correction is a SUM OF STEP FUNCTIONS of linear functions of the board: a one-hidden-layer threshold
network with scalar readout per class and a linear skip connection. It is the only DAG variant whose function class is
genuinely new relative to a tree, and it has 3 numbers per cell, so its capacity is tiny; and it is not linear in
growth (bddctx: 259k numbers for 601 nodes).

2.4 THEOREM (additive per-router tables = the main-effects model). Give each router r a table Delta_{r,k} per decision
k and predict with theta_shared.phi + sum_{r on path} Delta_{r, k_r(x)}.phi. A tree with the same routers is the FULL-
INTERACTION model: one table per joint decision (B^m of them for m levels, each trained on n / B^m examples); the
additive DAG has m B tables, each trained on n / B. Parameters and data per table are linear in the number of routers.
The additive model is exact when the conditional piece-square table is additive across the conditioning variables
(phase effect + king-side effect + ...), which is the natural first-order assumption; the tree's extra capacity is
their interaction, which no measurement here has shown to matter. This is Daume's augmentation per level with joint
training (theory 5.4 v), and the residual leaf of 1.1 is its depth-1 case.

2.5 What a DAG can do that a tree cannot, in one sentence: decouple the number of learned CONDITIONING VARIABLES
(routers) from the number of DATA FRAGMENTS (tables). A tree pays one fragmentation per router; the designs in 2.1-2.2
do not add conditioning (they share or deepen the gate); 2.3 adds conditioning at 3 numbers a cell; 2.4 adds it at d
numbers per decision without fragmenting the shared part.

2.6 Is the backprop framing right? Partly.
- Mechanically yes: a soft gate trained on Soft targets IS backprop through a mixture (HME, theory 6.1), and hard
  routing is its straight-through approximation. A router is a hidden unit with B states.
- What the framing misses is the fixed points. A hidden unit in a net sees every example; a router's children OWN
  disjoint data, so a partition that is a function of the label (lean, certainty) is self-consistent and stable
  (1.3). Nets have the same disease in mixtures (gates that learn the class), and cure it with load balancing (the
  user's Balanced), restricted gate inputs, or label-free gates (the user's cluster routers, which work). So the gate
  is not a feature detector; it is an allocator with a label leak.
- Nets also break symmetry with random initialisation over MANY units, and useless units are cheap. Here a unit is
  expensive (a fragmentation, 70-150 Elo for 40-120 cycles) and there are few of them. The right analogy for growth
  under those costs is boosting or cascade-correlation: add one unit at a time, fitted to the current residual (T4's
  direction), accept it only on held-out gain (the fresh merge test), shrink it towards zero (S1). The DAG that scales
  is the additive one of 2.4 with that loop.

2.7 What would have to be true for DAGs to pay. (a) Leaves must be the bottleneck: at present data per leaf is not
(1.6) and the routing headroom is 0.003, so neither sharing (2.1) nor deeper gates (2.2) can show. (b) There must be
conditioning variables beyond phase with additive effects of at least 0.01 nats, found by label-free routers; the
cheapest check is the first-order gain T4 measures along candidate partitions other than phase (per feature value: a
d x 3 running table of mean gradients at a flat LR), which is zero for a leaf optimal on every sub-region. If that
table's largest region gain is under 0.005 nats, no DAG, tree or router can help at this d, and growth must come from
the leaves' features, not from routing. (c) The accept/reject loop must exist for error routers: the ban on merges for
error routers removed the only guard against label-proxy partitions (1.3); the theory says the merges that collapsed
error trees were correct verdicts.


=====================================================================================================================
3. NEW SUGGESTIONS, RANKED BY EXPECTED VALUE AGAINST COST
=====================================================================================================================

Constraints kept: self-play only, zero knowledge, no hand features, linear in size, ply 1 first, one factor, lean.

S1. Shrinkage leaves (hierarchical prior). Idea: residual leaves with --residual-l2 > 0, or copies pulled towards the
    parent's shadow. Theory: Finkel-Manning hierarchical model; THEOREM 5.4 ii (never raises the training optimum) plus
    the noise reduction of sim_search_noise ("tree-shrunk" halves search regret in the toy) and the drift tracking of
    sim_residual. The pull makes a data-poor leaf predict as its parent until its data say otherwise, which removes
    the post-split transient's cause. Prediction: at ply 1 the tree never falls below flat after a split (today it
    loses 70-150 for 40-120 cycles), leaf noise is at or below flat's, late gain unchanged. Cost: nothing to build (the
    option exists; delta.step takes l2); the right l2 is unknown. Test: offline, the residual-leaves stream (its
    tour/games.csv rounds) at l2 in {1e-3, 1e-2, 1e-1} x inherit-sums on/off, scoring fresh rounds; then one ply-1
    line against the copy tree. Note the sim's l2 of 2e-3 per step was too weak to show anything; in the code l2 adds
    l2*w to the gradient per step, so the effective prior is l2 x (steps per weight), and the values to try are larger
    than one would guess.

S2. Calibration-invariant error targets. Idea: before computing the children's losses for the target, map each child's
    log-odds through its own affine recalibration (scale and offset per class, fitted online by an EMA of the
    calibration gradient on the child's stream), and compute the gap on the recalibrated predictions. THEOREM: two
    leaves whose recalibrated predictions coincide give a silent target, so partitions sustained only by temperature or
    lean differences are no longer fixed points (1.3); what remains is ranking (competence) difference. Prediction: U
    at cluster level (0.03), no starving child, T-P-Le and T-L-Le trees at or above flat at ply 1 (where gap already
    brought LR to 27-47 above), probit error trees no longer collapsing. Cost: one small change in delegation.rs
    (prediction_errors through a per-child cal layer), a per-child 4-number state. Test: offline in bddstudy's setup
    (the target arms exist: compare U, effective leaves, held-out), then one ply-1 line of T-P-Le and of T-L-Le.

S3. Growth as boosting: informed symmetry breaking. Idea: at a split, initialise child k as theta + eps a_k with a_k
    the per-region mean-gradient directions T4 already keeps, and install the probe as the router (--split-gradient-
    install). THEOREM (theory 6.3): the first-order loss change of the split is -eps sum_k a_k.(G_k - n_k mu) < 0, the
    steepest descent direction over region-wise moves; the children differ from the first example, by the data and not
    by a hash, so a consistent target is not silent and an LR router's first steps are not label noise (theory 4.2).
    Prediction: the split's transient halves; the router starts at the probe's partition. Cost: a few lines in
    trigger-src (the a_k tables exist). Test: offline in trigger's setup, four arms (init on/off x install on/off),
    then ply 1 against the stall trigger. If the transient does not shrink, the cost of a split is not where the
    children start, and (iv) holds for growth in general.

S4. The additive conditioning DAG (2.4). Idea: routers add B delta tables each, one shared base, no leaf
    multiplication; routers are label-free cluster routers accepted by a fresh test. Theory: 2.4; linear in growth by
    construction. Prediction: at one router it equals the residual tree (S1 with shrinkage); at m routers it matches a
    B^m-leaf tree where effects are additive and beats it where data per leaf binds. Cost: a new node kind; build
    after S1 shows shrinkage is right. Test: offline against the same-routers tree on offline-c15; ply 1 later.

S5. A variance metric beside the loss. Idea: per-position SD of the predicted log-odds across 5 adjacent versions on
    a fixed set of 50k positions, reported per model and per leaf by the experiment tools. Theory: play depends on
    the evaluator's ranking under noise (1.5); log-loss on game positions has never predicted play here (RESULTS
    section 1). Prediction: this metric ranks LR trees below flat where their play is below, and probit above LR.
    Cost: a short script over existing model files and a predict command that loads probit trees (chessai-predict
    predates them; hybrid9 has no predict). Test: compute on the finished runs' last 5 versions and correlate with the
    last-window Elo at each ply.

S6. Headroom census beyond phase (2.7 b). Idea: at a flat LR champion, the d x 3 table of mean gradients per feature
    value on a held-out round, and the first-order gain of a split on each single feature value; also the T4 statistic
    along a few label-free partitions other than phase (king position, pawn count by file, castling state). Theory:
    6.3; zero at a leaf optimal on every sub-region. Prediction: if every entry is under 0.005 nats, routing research
    at this feature set is closed by theory and growth must go into features (S7). Cost: a 2-hour script over existing
    positions with the LR weights (pure Python is feasible on 50k positions).

S7. Feature growth inside leaves (the other direction of "growth is the goal"). Idea: let d grow by adding pairwise
    (square, square) indicator features chosen by the same first-order statistic (the largest mean-gradient-correlated
    pair), zero-initialised, shrunk. Theory: 2.1 says sharing pays when d grows; cascade-correlation. This is where
    DAG sharing would start to matter. Not a hand feature: the candidate set is all pairs, the choice is by the data.
    Prediction: offline gain above 0.01 nats per 100 features if any interaction matters. Cost: a leaf kind; later.

S8. Restore the accept/reject loop for error routers (the fresh merge test), as the guard the theory says lean and
    certainty partitions need. The user banned merges for error routers on 11-08; this is a note for their decision,
    not a proposal to change a running line.

Not suggested: more router-split variants (2.1-2.2 bound them), per-leaf calibration at play (lost in play before, and
the toy says compensating noise restores the winner's curse), and any experiment on a 0.003-nat contrast without two
seeds and a ply-1 twin.


=====================================================================================================================

=====================================================================================================================

Minutes, existing models and games, no training:

     predict command for the current model kinds.
 T2. Calibration slope per leaf and |log-odds| of the leaf's region, T-P-Le (1.3's test). Leaves are already extracted
     in routing/leaves-etp-best/; needs a labelled decisive round and the calibration command on probit leaves.
 T3. T4's z against each fired split's realised held-out gain (1.2), eval-loss on the existing trigger-gradient models.
 T4. Drift commonality at more versions and lines (drift_corr.py, done for two).

Hours, offline training on existing streams:

 T7. Informed split initialisation in trigger's setup (S3), four arms.

=====================================================================================================================
5. WHICH ROUTERS TO FOCUS ON (the user's question of 11-10 ~23:10; three axes)
=====================================================================================================================

The evidence the choice rests on: cluster trees with LR leaves beat flat at ply 1 in every run (and at ply 2 in some);
NB error routers route like clustering (U 0.02-0.12) and, with probit leaves, are the only error trees above flat (40-48
at ply 2); LR error routers are evaluators in disguise (U 0.14-0.53; THEOREM 1.3 and the attractors); every routing
effect is 0.001-0.003 nats offline; a split costs 70-150 Elo for 40-120 cycles at ply 2; the 9-leaf copy tree's
sibling leaves move half together (drift_corr); residual leaves track common drift B times faster but are noisier.

5.1 The principle behind both recommendations
THEOREM (what a router can and cannot be). A router's output enters the loss only through which leaf predicts, so its
training signal is one of two things: a label-free statistic of x (cluster), or a comparison of the children's losses
(error). The second is a functional of p(y|x) (theory 1.4) and admits the lean and certainty fixed points for every
target (1.3). The first cannot form a label-proxy partition at all. So: routers should be LABEL-FREE in how they are
trained, and ERROR should enter only as an accept/reject test of the whole split on fresh data (the merge gate). This
is the boosting loop of 2.6, not the backprop loop. S2 (calibration-invariant targets) is the only error-routing
variant worth one test, because it removes both attractors by construction; if it fails, error routing is closed.

5.2 Design A: the hierarchical cluster tree (the proven base, made growable)
 Axis 1, when to split. A data threshold per leaf (the 1M that is used now is fine; break-even is passed within a few
   cycles, 1.6), with the FRESH-scored merge gate as the accept test, and the shrinkage of axis 3 so that a proposed
   split costs nothing while it is on trial. Not the stall trigger and not T4 as triggers: EVIDENCE (1.2) shows T4's
   first-order gain is the same at every leaf, so both are sample-size rules; the thing that varies is whether the
   split survives fresh scoring. Where it conditions: on the partition the NB cluster router finds in its window
   (phase-like, 88-99% agreement across runs). Trees with the stream-scored gate: EVIDENCE from growth-limits-p1 (at
   cycle 120, 23:20): the limit-4 and limit-8 lines reached 7-9 leaves by v100 and were merged back to 3 leaves by
   v120, and every tree was one leaf for its first 40-60 cycles. The stream test is biased towards the shadow for
   copied leaves (RESULTS section 4); the gate must score fresh rounds (--revise-on fresh) or these runs measure
   "limit 1 with interruptions".
 Axis 2, how the router is trained. NB cluster router (TAN is the same partition at more cost; LR cluster routers
   churn and are flat in effect: close them). Trained on its first window, then FROZEN: EVIDENCE that every working
   partition was fixed once made (single-split, cluster-error, T-P-Ne) and that continual retraining of a label-free
   router can only move the partition without an objective to improve. Hard routing (soft play-time blends cost 4-5x,
   and HME's gate has the same label leak). route-before-train is moot for a label-free router.
 Axis 3, how the leaves are trained. Hard-routed data. Leaves as residual deltas over the delegator's shadow with an L2
   pull towards zero (S1), inherited AdaGrad sums (so the first steps are not full-size, theory 7.7). The shadow
   keeps training on everything and carries the common half of the drift (drift_corr: 0.47-0.50 common in the 9-leaf
   tree); the delta carries the region part and is shrunk where data are thin. For PROBIT leaves this structure is
   built in: a copied probit leaf's prior IS its parent's posterior (a hierarchical prior with the parent as mean),
   which is why probit trees keep their gain at ply 2 and why T-P-Ne works; the probit knob is the widening of the
   copies' variance at a split (probit.rs has it: "a copy widened by noise^2 is the prior of a child"). EVIDENCE that
   the knob matters: probit at limit 4 is 39-93 behind F-P at both plies while limit 1 is ahead at ply 2: with no
   widening the children are as rigid as the parent and more leaves only fragment.
 Scaling. Linear: a split adds one router and B deltas; an example costs (depth + 1) LR steps (one per shadow on its
   path and one delta) and depth router evaluations; model size is one table per node. No B^depth terms.
 Predicted behaviour as it grows. No dip at a split (a child predicts as its parent until its data say otherwise);
   gain from the first phase split as now (30-100 at ply 2, 25-45 at ply 1); further splits inside a phase add little
   because siblings move together (drift_corr 0.5-0.7 for near-copies); the tree saturates at about 5-9 useful leaves
   on these features, and further growth needs richer leaf features (S7), where sharing starts to pay (2.1).
 Cheapest ply-1 tests, one factor each, two seeds, against the comparator named:
   A1. S1: T-L-Nc-merge limit 4 with --leaf-residual --residual-inherit-sums --residual-l2 X (X from section 6)
       against the same recipe with copies. Reads: late gap, the noise metric (S5), leaves kept.
   A2. Fresh gate: T-L-Nc-merge limit 4 with --revise-on fresh against the stream gate. Reads: leaves kept over time,
       late gap. growth-limits-p1 is the stream-gate baseline for this.
   A3. Probit widening: T-P-Nc-merge limit 4 with the copies' variance widened at splits against no widening.

5.3 Design B: the additive conditioning DAG (the growth direction)
 Axis 1. A new ROUTER (conditioning variable) is proposed when the first-order gain along a candidate label-free
   partition (T4's statistic, computed along several probes: phase, king zone, pawn structure clusters, material
   class) exceeds a bar relative to the current model, and it is kept only if a fresh window shows gain. The S6
   census tells first whether any such candidate exists at this feature set.
 Axis 2. Each router is a frozen NB cluster router over its own probe; hard decisions; routers are independent of one
   another (no tree of routers), so a position's path is a vector of m decisions.
 Axis 3. One shared base table trained on everything; per router r and decision k a delta table Delta_{r,k}, shrunk
   to zero, trained on the examples with decision k; prediction = base + sum of the path's deltas (2.4; Daume per
   level; the residual leaf is its m = 1 case). Inherited sums for new deltas. No data fragmentation of the base, and
   each delta sees n/B examples however many routers there are.
 Scaling. Linear in routers: m B tables, (m + 1) LR steps per example, m router evaluations.
 Predicted behaviour. Equals Design A at one router; at m routers it matches a B^m-leaf tree where the conditional
   piece-square table is additive across the conditioning variables, and beats it where data per leaf binds. If the
   S6 census finds no candidate above 0.005 nats, Design B has nothing to add on these features and should wait for S7.
 Cheapest test. Offline first (a new node kind): Design B with two routers against Design A with two levels, on
   bddstudy's testbed; then a ply-1 line against A1's winner.

5.4 Doors to close, with the reason
 - LR error routers, any target, any leaf: THEOREM 1.3 (lean and certainty attractors inside the LR class), EVIDENCE
   in every run (U 0.14-0.53; never above flat at ply 2; probit leaves 100-140 below). Closed. Exception: one S2 test.
 - Best, Soft and Balanced targets: inconsistent (theory 1.2-1.5) and subject to the same attractors. Closed.
 - Error routing as the way to train routers in general (NB included): it buys nothing over clustering when it works
   (NB error routers route like clustering) and risks the attractors when it does not. Keep error as the ACCEPT test.
 - LR cluster routers: churn, flat in effect in every run. Closed.
 - TAN routers: the same partition as NB at more cost (single-split 99 against 88 is noise). Keep NB.
 - Router splits, home DAGs, path cells: bounded by 2.1-2.3 at 0.001-0.003 nats. Closed until d grows.
 - Soft routing at play: the user's decision; the theory agrees (cost, and the gate is an allocator).
 - The stall trigger and T4 as triggers (both are sample-size rules in the league); T4's directions stay for S3 and S6.
 - Copy-on-split for LR beyond one split without shrinkage: the dip is the cost and the siblings are near-copies.
 - Forgetting at splits (F > 1): wrong for CNBP (theory 2.2), unnecessary for LR with inherited sums.
 - Stream-scored merge gates: biased (RESULTS section 4); use fresh.

5.5 The two ply-1 runs started at 22:52
 small-field-x4-p1 (F-L x4 against T-L-Tc-merge x4, ply-1 field, self-games, 10 rounds a cycle, ~320k positions a
 cycle). At cycle 95 (curtab, current@1 against F-L#1, 600-700 games a cell, about +-25): the three other F-L are -3
 to 17, the four trees -9 to 21: level. Its trees were one leaf until v30-60 and have 3 leaves since. What to look for:
 (a) the seed spread among the four F-L (the noise floor; 5-17 now) against the spread among the four trees; (b)
 whether the trees separate from flat after cycle ~150-250 as in every ply-2 run (small-field: from cycle ~110 at
 ply 1); (c) whether there is ANY dip after the split at ply 1: the ply-2 runs lost 70-150 at a split; if ply 1 shows
 none, the dip is a search effect and S1's ply-1 test must be read on the late gap and the noise metric, not on a dip;
 (d) the partition (leafsim/what.py): phase-like or not when the training games are ply-1 games.
 growth-limits-p1 (T-L-Nc-merge at limits 1/4/8, 2 seeds, 18 rounds a cycle). At cycle 120 all six lines are within
 -9 to 9 of limit 1 seed 1 (1,300-1,440 games a cell). Leaf counts from the model files: 1 leaf to v40-60, 3 by
 v60-80, 7-9 at v100 for the limit-4 and limit-8 lines, and 3 again at v120 for all: the stream-scored gate undid
 the second-level splits. What to look for: (e) whether the limit-4/8 lines ever HOLD more than 3 leaves (if not, the
 run compares limit 1 with itself and says nothing about growth); (f) the Elo of the limit-4/8 lines in the windows
 where they held 7-9 leaves (v100-v115) against their 3-leaf windows: a direct read of the second-level splits' cost
 at ply 1; (g) the merge log's stream gains for the undone splits (biased towards merging). Recommendation: when it
 is rerun or extended, the gate should be --revise-on fresh, and the limit-8 line should log its leaf count per cycle.


FILES. summary.txt (this), sim_search_noise.py/.out, sim_residual.py/.out, sim_cal_routing.py/.out, who_wins.out
(who_wins_share_h9.py is an unused copy pointing at hybrid9, which has no predict command), drift_corr.py/.out,
routing/ (run_routing.sh, *.routing.out, leaves-etp-best/, drift/ with the extracted leaves and the per-version
leaves of the copy and single-split trees).

=====================================================================================================================
6. THE S1 SWEEP, WHAT IT CHANGES, AND THE PLAN FOR THE NIGHT OF 11-10/11 (written ~00:05)
=====================================================================================================================

6.1 S1 offline l2 sweep (l2sweep/: train_arms.sh, fresh_eval.sh, noise.sh, analyse.out, noise_table.out)
Setup: bddstudy's testbed (offline-c15, 60 rounds of ~115k ply-2 positions; residual-leaves' own pool had been
pruned), chessai-hybrid6, T-L-Nc limit 4, threshold 300k, --reset-leaves inf, seed 1; fresh scoring = model N on
round N before training on it; noise = per-position SD across versions 55-59 on round 59. Arm minus flat, nats:
  copies            all -0.0077, rounds 51-59 -0.0138; after each split -0.003 to -0.015 (NO dip, a gain at once);
                    noise 0.224 against flat's 0.231.
  residual, inherited sums, l2 0 / 1e-3 / 1e-2 / 1e-1 / 1:  all +0.0044 / +0.0044 / +0.0042 / +0.0033 / +0.0004;
                    late (51-59) -0.0010 to -0.0028; after the first split +0.009 to +0.013; noise 0.30-0.34.
  residual, fresh sums: +0.023 to +0.0005 (l2 1), noise 0.31-0.43.
EVIDENCE verdict: on a fixed ply-2 stream, copies have no post-split dip and flat's noise; the implemented residual
leaf is worse and 30-50% noisier, and shrinkage moves it towards flat, never towards copies. The toy's drift gain does
not appear (the stream is 4 round robins of one cycle: little drift). S1 is dropped in this form; the theory-2 claim
that stands is only the noise one (1.1). Design A is revised to COPIES with inherited sums (what the league runs).

6.2 Where the league's post-split dip (70-150 Elo at ply 2 for 40-120 cycles) can come from, if not from the leaves
(a) Not from how leaves learn on a fixed stream: 6.1. (b) Not from ply 1: small-field-x4-p1 and growth-limits-p1 show
no dip at ply 1 (the trees were level with flat throughout), and the old small-field's ply-1 quarters had none either
while its ply-2 quarters did (-15, -12, 9, 28). So the dip is a PLY-2 PLAY effect. (c) Not from uneven independent
leaf noise (1.5, and the noise is now measured equal). What is left, ranked: (1) across-leaf comparisons inside the
min over replies: a reply that changes phase (a capture or trade) is scored by another leaf with its own intercept
and scale; at ply 2 nearly every move has such a reply, at ply 1 only the capture moves themselves are affected, so
a young split's leaf-to-leaf offset enters almost every ply-2 move value. CONJECTURE; the per-leaf calibration that
lost in play before (RESULTS section 1) argues against a simple scale fix, but that was fitted on few ply-1 own games.
(2) The gate's churn under the stream test (split, merge, split: growth-limits-p1 at ply 1 shows the oscillation),
which makes the router restart with a hash window each time; it is absent in residual-leaves and trigger-gradient
(no merges), which still dipped, so it is not the whole story. (3) Calibration on 600 ply-1 own games: the slope
misfits a fresh split more than a mature one (diagnostic (a) found +-30 noise, with 70-Elo outliers). Cheap test:
replay residual-leaves' copy tree v60-v150 against F-L at the same versions at ply 1 and ply 2 in one arena, with
the seam statistic of fs/optimism (value jump at phase crossings) for young and old versions; if the ply-2 deficit
tracks the seam jump, (1).

6.3 What small-field-x4-p1 implies for routing at ply 1
Facts: F-L x4 against T-L-Tc-merge x4, pure ply-1 field, 190 cycles: trees -18 to 13 against F-L#1, flat seeds -11
to 14; the trees held 3 leaves from v30-60. growth-limits-p1 (340 cycles): all limits within -20 to 11 of limit 1.
THEOREM (scale): a 30-100 Elo ply-2 routing gain is 15-50 on the ply-1 scale (slope 0.50), and the four flat seeds
span 25 with ~700 games a cell: the ply-1 league cannot resolve a routing effect of the size ever measured.
CONJECTURE (size): the gain itself is smaller on ply-1 games: 43% draws against 10%, so the outcome signal per
position (|log-odds|) is smaller and so is the phase partition's gain in nats. Both say the same thing: ply 1 is a
cheap screen for LARGE effects (leaf kind, data source, a 40-Elo loss to repair), not for routing. The ply-1 gains
seen before came from models trained on ply-2 or mixed games and measured at ply 1: those are ply-2 evaluators, and
the gain was theirs. Implication for the plan: tonight's ply-1 experiments must target effects of 40 Elo or more on
the ply-1 scale, or be mechanism readouts (leaves held, merges) rather than Elo contrasts; routing growth has to be
judged at ply 2.

6.4 Plan for tonight (specs in plans/; the coordinator creates and launches; every one entry_ply 1 unless stated,
self_games, scaled plateau, no references, hybrid9 tournament; set the training binary with train_binary.py to
chessai-hybrid6 before cycle 1)
 P1. probit-widen-p1 (plans/probit-widen-p1.json). T-P-Nc-merge limit 4, threshold 1M, --split-noise 0 (comparator)
     / 0.03 / 0.1, two seeds each, seed 14, 12 rounds a cycle, min_cycles 200. One factor (the copies' prior
     widening at a split; the flag exists in hybrid6: child = parent posterior + N(0, s^2) in quadrature; the probit
     prior SD is 0.3, so 0.03 is a tenth and 0.1 a third of it). Why first: the effect to repair is large at ply 1
     (limit-4 probit trees 26-47 BELOW F-P at ply 1 in split-growth-probit, 39-93 at ply 2), so the screen can see
     it; it is Design A's probit knob (5.2). Prediction: with widening the limit-4 trees stop losing to limit 1; if
     0.1 overshoots they dip after each split and recover. Read: the four-leaf lines against the comparator from
     cycle ~100; leaf counts. Cost: probit is ~2.4x LR a game; ~25-40 CPU-s a cycle.
 P2. gate-fresh-p1 (plans/gate-fresh-p1.json). T-L-Nc-merge limit 4, threshold 1M, --revise 0 with --revise-on
     stream (the recipe, comparator) against --revise-on fresh, two seeds each, seed 15, 12 rounds a cycle. One
     factor: only --revise-on (T-L-Nc-fresh's recipe also changes --revise to 2; not that). Read: a MECHANISM
     readout, not Elo: leaves held per version (model files), merges per 50 cycles, and no loss against the
     comparator. Cheap (~10-15 CPU-s a cycle). The offline version of this test is running now (6.5); if it shows
     the fresh gate holds the splits the stream gate undoes, P2 can be skipped tonight and the flag adopted.
 P3. gate-fresh-p2 (plans/gate-fresh-p2.json): the same two-line contrast at PLY 2 (entry_ply 2, 9 rounds a cycle,
     seed 16, min_cycles 300), where routing gains are visible. ~66 CPU-s a cycle, so 300 cycles is ~5.5 CPU-h: only
     if the night's budget allows a third run; otherwise the first ply-2 run after the user wakes.
 Not tonight: a growth rerun with the fresh gate at ply 1 (6.3: unreadable), S1 in any form, more LR cluster trees
 at ply 1, a frozen-router or data-threshold-trigger line (no existing flag: --split-rule has only worse and
 stall-high; a "threshold" rule and a router freeze would need code: two small changes in delegation.rs, worth a
 build agent only after P3 shows the fresh gate holds leaves at ply 2).
 Diagnostics worth doing tonight on finished runs (minutes each): (i) the ply-1/ply-2 arena of 6.2 on residual-
 leaves' versions v60-150 and v300-399; (ii) growth-limits-p1's Elo in the windows where its limit-4/8 lines held
 7-9 leaves against their 3-leaf windows (RESULTS already has the oscillation); (iii) leafsim/what.py on small-field-
 x4-p1's final trees: is the ply-1 partition phase-like?
 On the 100-cycle plateau floor: too short for tree lines. The trees in both ply-1 runs were one leaf until v30-60,
 and every ply-2 tree gain formed 40-120 cycles after its split; a stop 25 cycles after the last crowning can land
 at 130-155, before the split has paid. For tree lines use min_cycles 200, or better, a floor of 100 cycles after
 the line's last SPLIT (needs a driver change); the specs above say 200 (300 at ply 2).

6.5 Offline fresh-gate test (running; results appended below when done)
Result (l2sweep/analyse_gate.out; same testbed and recipe as 6.1, --revise 0): the STREAM gate split at v10, 20, 28,
36, 46, 54 and merged 6 times, ending with ONE leaf and a fresh loss of -0.0006 against flat (nothing kept); the FRESH
gate (--revise-on fresh, --revise 0, and also --revise 2) never merged and is identical to the no-merge copies: 9
leaves, -0.0077 overall and -0.0138 late. EVIDENCE: the stream test undoes splits that are gaining on fresh data (its
bias towards the shadow, RESULTS section 4, reproduced here), and the fresh test keeps them. So P2 is answered
offline: adopt --revise-on fresh for every merge-gated recipe; the league's "merge" lines to date (single-split,
split-growth, growth-limits-p1) measured a gate that was mostly collapsing the trees it tested. The remaining question
for a league run is whether the fresh gate also rejects BAD splits in play, which only ply 2 can show: P3.
CPU for the gate test: about 4 CPU-minutes. Session total about 35 CPU-minutes.

6.6 Overnight diagnostics (00:20-00:50; growth_leaves_elo.py/.out, subsets/shares.out, plans/diag-dip.json,
    plans/P3-case.txt; about 6 CPU-minutes)
(b) growth-limits-p1, ply 1: each limit-4/8 line's score against the field (current versions only) grouped by the
