    2-30 cycles). Elo from the score, 12,000-20,000 games a bucket, +-5-6 at 95%: 3 leaves -0.6 / +1.5 / 0.0 / +0.3;
    5 leaves -4.9 / -0.5 / -4.0 / +1.6; 7 leaves +3.6 / -0.5 / +1.4 / +2.4; 9 or more -4.5 / -3.0 / -3.8 / -4.2 (the
    four lines). EVIDENCE: at ply 1 a tree with 9 leaves plays exactly as the same line with 3, and the gate's churn
    costs nothing visible. A leaf count is not a strength at ply 1.
(c) small-field-x4-p1's final trees (T-L-Tc-merge #1-4, v189): chessai-predict cannot load TAN routers, so the
    partition was read with `routing` on held-out subsets by phase (pieces >= 24 / 12-23 / < 12) and by material
    balance (|bal| <= 1 / >= 4). All four seeds route identically (shares 35.4/24.1/40.5, per-region losses equal to
    three decimals, routing regret 0.0000). Subsets: opening 91.5% to one child, middlegame 92.1% to another, endgame
    83.0% to the third; material balance does not separate (even 26/65/10, lopsided 35/13/51, i.e. follows phase).
    The ply-2 reference (single-split T-L-Tc v999) is the same kind of partition (84/80/99.5% by phase) with a
    different boundary (the ply-1 tree sends 17% of endgames to its middlegame leaf). EVIDENCE: the ply-1 trees
    found the phase partition, specialised their leaves (per-region winner losses 0.12 / 0.40 / 0.59 with sibling
    gaps of 0.05-0.08) and gained nothing in ply-1 play. So the phase split's gain is a ply-2 play effect, like the
    dip: both appear only where the search compares positions across leaves.
(a) The dip arena is drafted at plans/diag-dip.json: residual-leaves' copy tree and F-L at v60, 90, 120, 150 (the
    dip: the tree trailed by 32-47 at ply 2) and v300, 350, 399 (level), every entry at ply 1 and ply 2, own
    calibration, random-3 as the only reference, 15 double round robins of 29 players (~12,000 games). Cost: the
    diag arenas ran at 0.010 CPU-s a game with random players; tree-against-tree at ply 2 is heavier, so budget
    3-6 CPU-minutes. Read: tree minus flat at the same version, ply 1 against ply 2, dip versions against late ones.
    The spec passes "--save-games" to the tournament through the arena's flags so that the seam statistic can be
    run afterwards; if the arena's tournament refuses the pair, drop it and skip the seams. Seam command (the
    optimism study's tool; its chessai-predict loads NB-routed LR trees):
      cd /home/iv/dev/chessai && nice -n 19 python3 -I models/from-scratch/optimism/seams.py \
        leagues/arenas/diag/dip/pool leagues/arenas/diag/dip/tour/players.txt --ref flat@399-2 \
        --entries tree@60-2 tree@90-2 tree@120-2 tree@150-2 tree@300-2 tree@350-2 tree@399-2
    (player names as the arena's players.txt spells them; the tool wants decisive games of the tree entries and a
    flat reference that did not choose their moves). Prediction under 6.2 (1): the seam residual (tree overrates
    the side that just crossed leaves) is larger for the dip versions than for the late ones, and the ply-2 deficit
    tracks it; under (3) (calibration) the dip closes when both are calibrated at ply 2 (diag (a) found +-30 noise).
(P3) The case for the ply-2 gate experiment is in plans/P3-case.txt.

6.7 The dip arena (leagues/arenas/diag/dip, 11-11 ~01:10) and what it says about the dip's cause
Facts (tree minus F-L at the same version, Elo, +-45-51 per version): dip versions v60-150 at ply 1 -5, -6, 14, 39
(mean 10 +-23), at ply 2 -24, -50, -84, 1 (mean -39 +-25); late v300-399 at ply 1 65, 38, 83 (62 +-26), at ply 2
88, 27, 75 (63 +-29). Ply-2 gap minus ply-1 gap: -50 +-33 in the dip, 2 +-39 late. The dip is confirmed on the same
versions, at ply 2 only, and it is gone by v150. Seams (optimism's tool, ref flat@399-2): the dip versions' seam
residual is -0.012/-0.019 (v60), then positive from v90 on and LARGEST late (+0.007 to +0.013), with FEWER crossings
in the dip (3.6-6.6% of positions) than late (11%). The tree's own-side optimism on its own ply-2 positions (actual
minus calibrated, tree minus ref): -0.137, -0.057, -0.041, -0.030 (v60-150), then -0.018, -0.011, -0.012; the seam
tool's residual slope on the tree's calibrated values: 0.60, 0.86, 1.04, 1.15, then 1.60-1.66, against 1.9-2.5 for
the ref throughout. Also: flat's ply-1 rating FALLS with version (737 to 625) while the tree's holds (708-746).

Reading of the ranked causes of 6.2:
(1) Across-leaf comparisons at seams, as measured by crossings: REFUTED as the dip's cause. The crossing residual is
    positive (the tree is, if anything, pessimistic about the side that just crossed) and larger late, when the tree
    leads; and the dip versions cross less, not more. The young tree's leaves are still near-copies (the partition's
    boundaries hardly matter two plies apart), so there is little to exploit at a seam.
(2) Gate churn: absent in this line (no merges), so not this dip.
(3) Calibration of a young split, applied at ply 2: SUPPORTED, in a sharper form. The cal-own slope is fitted on 600
    ply-1 own games; on the tree's own ply-2 positions the young tree is HOT (residual slope 0.60 at v60, i.e. its
    calibrated log-odds are 1.7x too large) and flat is COLD (1.9-2.5) on the same positions, and both play a
    log-odds softmax at a fixed temperature of 0.1. The league calibration confound measured 132 Elo per unit of
    ln(slope ratio); ln(2.2 / 0.6) = 1.3 would be ~170 Elo against the tree at v60 and ln(2.2 / 1.6) = 0.3 about 40
    late, where the tree leads by 63-88 (so its ranking edge is larger than it looks). The magnitudes are rough
    because the seam tool's slope on OWN games also contains selection optimism (the winner's curse of the tree's
    own search, 0.137 in probability at v60), but the direction and the ordering over versions match the dip and
    its disappearance. Why a young split is hot at ply 2 while flat is cold: the children start as copies and then
    each trains on a third of the stream with inherited AdaGrad sums, so each leaf's weights keep moving at the
    parent's step size on a third of the data: their log-odds scale grows faster than flat's in the region the
    search explores (the off-game positions a ply-2 search scores, PATH-FORWARD (c)), and a slope fitted on ply-1
    own games does not see those positions. CONJECTURE for the mechanism; the slope difference itself is EVIDENCE.
So the lead is: the dip is an artefact of a fixed-temperature softmax on values whose scale was fitted at the other
ply, and a young split's scale moves fastest. If true, the dip is not a property of trees at all but of the play
policy, and a scale-free policy (NEW-DESIGN, Open) removes the whole class. Two cheap arenas separate it from the
alternative that the young tree's RANKING is worse at ply 2:
  plans/diag-dip-cal2.json: the same 14 versions calibrated on 600 own games at depth 2 (diag (a)'s method); if the
    dip versions' ply-2 deficit closes towards their ply-1 gap, (3) in its calibration form.
  plans/diag-dip-cold.json: the same arena at softmax temperature 0.02 (near argmax, scale almost irrelevant) with the
    depth-1 calibrations; if the dip shrinks, (3) in its policy form; if it stays, the young tree's ranking of moves is
    worse at ply 2 and the policy is not the cause. (The standard arena's policy must be confirmed as log-odds-softmax
    at 0.1; the spec's description says what to do if the default differs.)
Each is ~1 minute of wall time as the first arena was; the cal2 twin adds 14 calibrations at depth 2 (a few minutes).

The slot tonight: fill it with these two arenas, then nothing. Reasons: P2 is settled offline (6.5), agreed; the
ply-1 screen cannot see routing effects (6.3, 6.6 b-c), so a new ply-1 routing experiment would be wasted; and the
only 40-Elo-at-ply-1 effect in sight (probit limit 4) is already running in probit-widen-p1b. If the cold-policy arena
shrinks the dip, the morning's decision is a scale-free policy for the new league, which is a driver change, not an
experiment. The remaining open fact worth a ply-1 line later is flat's falling ply-1 rating over versions (737 to
625) while the tree holds: the flat ply-2 evaluator loses ply-1 skill as it specialises and the tree does not; a
mechanism readout rather than a contrast, and not for tonight.

6.8 The twin arenas (dip-cal2, dip-cold; 11-11 ~01:40): the dip is real, the late lead is an artefact
Facts (tree minus F-L, group means +-23-30): standard (depth-1 own cal, T 0.1): dip 10 / -39, late 62 / 63 (ply 1 /
ply 2); depth-2 own cal, T 0.1: dip -2 / -60, late -9 / -29; depth-1 cal, T 0.02 (near argmax): dip -5 / -39, late
1 / -42. The coordinator's reading is right on both counts:
(a) The dip survives calibration at the right ply and near-argmax play, so the young tree's ply-2 RANKING is worse,
    not its scale. With (1) seams and (3) calibration refuted and (2) absent, the remaining candidate is PATH-FORWARD's
    (c): the positions a ply-2 search scores that never occur in games (after a bad reply), where a young leaf has
    the least data and flat shares its weights across phases; nothing in a game-position metric (loss, noise, seams)
    can see them. Test: the rank-test command (ranks every legal move of sampled positions against playout values)
    on dip and late versions of tree and flat, the tree's rank quality on REPLY positions being the prediction;
    cost depends on playouts, so start with few positions.
(b) The late 60-Elo lead at both plies exists only under the standard setup; cal2 and cold both put the late tree
    level at ply 1 and 29-42 behind at ply 2. THEOREM (why T 0.1 can manufacture a lead): with a log-odds softmax at a
    fixed temperature, a model's effective temperature is T divided by its calibrated scale, so two models of equal
    ranking but different scales play with different randomness, and the match result measures that difference
    (the league calibration confound, 132 Elo per ln(slope ratio), is the same effect); a depth-1 own calibration
    sets each model's scale on a different distribution from the ply-2 positions it then plays, and a split changes
    a tree's scale (6.7). Near-argmax play removes the scale from the result, and alpha-beta plays argmax, so the
    cold measurement is the one that bears on the goal. EVIDENCE that it is general: old-growth at T 0.1 (the old
    champion lineages' later versions beat their earlier ones by 127-163) still has flat LR beating every lineage's
    last version by 31-60; and top-champions-1 at T 0.1 had flat LR 46 above the best old tree champion.
Which earlier gains are at risk: every tree-vs-flat ply-2 verdict was taken at T 0.1 with depth-1 own calibration:
single-split (LR cluster trees 88-99, probit 75-77 and T-P-Ne 44), split-growth (35-53), small-field (20-29),
split-growth-probit (20-51). The LR cluster trees are the most exposed (their scale moves most with a split, 6.7);
probit's posterior shrinkage keeps its scale steadier, so its gains are the likelier to survive, but they were
measured the same way. The cheapest decisive re-measurement is one cold arena of the final versions:
plans/cold-trees.json (16 entries from five experiments, each at ply 1 and 2, T 0.02, 15 double round robins, ~2-4
minutes of wall time); read each tree against its own experiment's flat line. If no tree beats its flat under T 0.02
at ply 2, the routing programme has no measured gain left and 5.2's Design A loses its base; if the probit trees
keep theirs, the leaf kind is the lever and routing is a probit-only story for now.
League design: (i) rate at near-argmax (T 0.02) or with a scale-free policy (softmax on ranks, or jitter on ranks),
and calibrate at the rating ply or not at all for the policy; (ii) keep exploration out of the rating: diversity
should come from randomised openings (the first 4-6 plies uniformly random; the random players already exist, and
it is zero-knowledge), not from temperature, because a deterministic pair otherwise replays one game; (iii) the
training games are the rating games in the new design, so (ii) is also what keeps the training stream diverse.
probit-widen-p1b: its verdict is taken at T 0.1 with depth-1 calibration, and widening changes the leaves' scale
(wider posteriors predict cooler), so part of any effect will be temperature. Read it with a cold arena of its final
versions before believing it; better, give new experiments "--temperature 0.02" in their tournament flags (the
driver passes --policy and --temperature through, leagues.py:2614). P3: the premise (a late tree lead to protect
with a fresh gate) is now in doubt; cold-trees decides first, and if P3 runs it must rate at T 0.02.

6.9 After cold-trees (11-11 ~02:15): the path forward for an alpha-beta evaluator
Facts (cold-trees and its T 0.1 twin warm-trees; tree minus its own flat, 95% +-20-23 for the group means): at ply
2 under T 0.02 the five gaining LR trees are -17 and the five probit trees 9; at T 0.1 the same models are 4 and 62.
At ply 1 cold: LR 18, probit -31. Also cold: flat LR from a ply-2-only field 96 above the best old tree champion,
flat probit 55 above the champions' mean; the old lineages' growth is real (149-244) but flat LR is 48-104 above
each lineage's last version. No tree beats its flat at ply 2 under near-argmax play.

1. Does routing keep a measured case? Not at ply 2, and that is the ply that matters. What remains: (i) the ply-1
   cold LR gain, 18 +-20 (T-L-Nc-merge 36, sg L1 30), the evaluator alone, not significant; (ii) the fresh-scored
   offline gain of the phase split on game positions, 0.004-0.014 nats (6.1, 6.5), which is real and does not reach
   ply-2 play. THEOREM (why an offline gain on game positions need not reach the search): a ply-2 search scores
   mostly positions that never occur in games (every reply, good and bad); a routed tree fits each region with that
   region's data only, so its weights for features rare in a region (a missing queen in the opening) rest on little
   data, while flat's rest on every phase's; the log-loss on game positions cannot see this, the minimax over
   replies is decided by it. So routing optimises the measured objective and not the search's. Larger data per leaf
   does not help (single-split had 245M positions), and deeper search makes it worse (more off-game positions per
   decision). CONJECTURE, with one cheap test before parking: plans/residual-cold.json, residual-leaves' F-L, copy
   tree and residual tree at v150, 300, 399 at ply 2, T 0.02. The residual tree shares its base over all regions
   (the shadow trains on everything), which is exactly what the theorem says the search needs, even though it lost
   on game-position loss (6.1). If residual beats copy cold, the additive direction (2.4, base plus small deltas)
   keeps a case and the next step is a shrunk additive model rated cold; if not, park routing: trees, DAGs and
   routers, all of it, until the evaluator's features change.
2. Levers for the goal, ranked by measured size and robustness:
   a. Field and data (ply-2-only fields: 424 for LR, 754 for probit at ply 2, measured at T 0.1): by far the largest
      effect; confirm it cold with a replay of the lr-settings pair arena at T 0.02 (cheap).
   b. The ply of the training games: models are good at the ply they train at (section 1), d4-trained LR beat
      models trained on 4.5-8.7x the positions at matched depth, and the goal is beyond ply 2. The measurement that
      defines the goal is missing: nothing has been rated at ply 3. plans/ply3-cold.json rates the best flats and
      the old d4-trained lineage at ply 2 and 3, near-argmax (10 rounds, extend if wide). Cost of the lever: ply-3
      and ply-4 games are 7-70x ply-2 games; the thunderdome's one-ply-per-model design is the right vehicle.
   c. Learning rate: 0.0125 best in every window of lr-settings, with the trend not yet turned; a one-factor line at
      0.006 is cheap and may be worth 50-100.
   d. Leaf kind, cold: flat probit against flat LR has never been rated head to head cold at ply 2 or 3 (ply3-cold
      includes both); probit costs 2.4x per node, which matters for deeper search.
   e. Measurement: rate at T 0.02 or scale-free, randomised openings for diversity, calibrate at the rating ply.
      Not a strength lever, but every verdict above a. was taken without it.
   f. Features: every model is linear in one-hot squares; a search evaluator beyond ply 2 needs interactions. The
      FM model (pairwise, low rank) exists and was never rated cold; the first-order growth of S7 is the principled
      way to add pairs. This is where "growth" should go once routing is parked.
   First experiment, when the user resumes ply 2: a ply-2-only field, rated at T 0.02, F-L at rate 0.0125 against
   0.006 (one factor, two seeds), with the ply3-cold arena having told us first whether LR or probit is the leaf
   to build on. Every new experiment should rate cold.
3. probit-widen-p1b: moot for the goal. It tests a routing knob at ply 1 at T 0.1; cold, the probit trees are -31
   at ply 1 and 9 at ply 2. Recommend the user stops it in the morning; its versions can still be read cold if
   wanted. Not stopped by me.
4. Cheap diagnostics tonight: ply3-cold (the goal's measurement, 5-15 min), residual-cold (routing's last test,
   ~2 min), a cold replay of the lr-settings pair arena (confirms lever a; its spec is leagues/specs/arenas/ply1-
   lr-settings.json with plies [2] and the T 0.02 pair), and the FM champion against flat LR cold if an FM
   champion is on disk.

6.10 After the four cold tests (11-11 ~02:50): levers re-ranked and the first experiment
Facts (all T 0.02): residual - copy 18 +-36, residual - flat -45, copy - flat -63 at ply 2: ROUTING PARKED (trees,
DAGs, routers), by the criterion of 6.9. Field composition: the ply-2-only rerun beats the mixed original by 542
+-38 at ply 2 (424 at T 0.1). Learning rate: GONE cold (0.025 23, 0.05 -18, 0.1 -8 against 0.0125, +-45; at T 0.1
they were -29 to -118): the rate "effect" was temperature, so lr-settings-new's ~200 lead over leaf-sources-new's
F-L lines (both ply-2-only) must be the field's quality (weak NB and self-play lines' games in the stream). Ply 3:
the old depth-4 lineage with 50/50 search labels is -46 at ply 2 and 63 at ply 3 against the best ply-2-field LR
(swing 109 +-52); the label line of lr-settings-new is 48 +-46 at ply 2; flat probit is -177 at ply 3 and FM -307;
ply 3 is 480-610 above ply 2 for LR.

(a) Levers, ranked:
 1. Which games the evaluator trains on: the ply of the games (542) and the strength of the field (~200, EVIDENCE by
    elimination, plus "a strong player lifts every line by ~100" and 2.7x per position for games against stronger
    opponents). The thunderdome's "train on games of players at or above own Elo minus a margin" is this lever.
 2. The depth the data was made with: search labels and deeper games carry to ply 3 (the d4mix swing), as
    specialisation predicts; it is the lever that defines "beyond ply 2". Cost: depth-4 games 70x ply-2 games;
    depth-2 labels 12x the training CPU (training is a small share for flat LR).
 3. Leaf kind: flat LR, decided (probit -177 at ply 3 and 2.4x the cost; FM -307).
 4. Measurement: T 0.02 or scale-free, randomised openings, calibration at the rating ply: a prerequisite, not a
    strength lever; every earlier verdict without it is suspect (rate, routing).
 5. Features (pair growth by the first-order statistic, S7): the only model-side lever left; long-term, untested.
 Closed: learning rate (no effect cold), routing (6.9), probit for depth, FM as built.
(b) The first experiment for when ply 2+ resumes: plans/labels-p2-cold.json, "labels-p2-cold". F-L with result
    labels (comparator, 2 seeds) against F-L with --label-depth 2 --label-result 0.5 (2 seeds), in a ply-2-only
    field of its own games (entry_ply 2, self_games, no references), rated at T 0.02, 15 rounds a cycle, scaled
    plateau with min_cycles 200. One factor: the labels. Trains and is rated at ply 2; the verdict that matters is a
    final cold arena of the four champions at ply 3 (10-20 rounds, minutes), with ply 2 beside it. Why this one:
    it is the cheapest test of lever 2 (existing flags, ply-2 cycles of ~26 CPU-s for flat LR plus the labels' 12x
    training CPU, about 2-4 CPU-h for 300 cycles), it has a cold +48 at ply 2 already, and the d4mix evidence says
    the gain grows with the rating ply. Prediction: labels lead by 30-60 at ply 2 and 80-150 at ply 3. If it wins,
    the follow-up is B: the same contrast with ply-3 games (a ply-3 field, ~7x the game cost), the thunderdome's
    one-ply-per-model design being the vehicle; if it loses at ply 3, labels are not the carrier and B is next.
(c) One cheap diagnostic tonight that could change the choice: plans/ply3-labels-cold.json, the label line's final
    version against the 0.0125 and 0.05 lines and the d4mix champion at ply 3 only (20 rounds, minutes). If the
    label line leads at ply 3 by well over its +48 at ply 2, labels-p2-cold is confirmed; if it is level, labels do
    not carry and the first experiment should be B (ply-3 games) despite the cost. Nothing else cheap bears on the
    choice; the field-quality candidate (lever 1b) needs a run, and the thunderdome will test it anyway.

6.11 Re-plan after ply3-labels-cold, selfplay-depth-ply3-cold and probit-widen-p1b (11-11 ~03:20; the user's
     standing permission: launch without them when little else runs)
Facts (T 0.02). Labels do not carry: at ply 3 the depth-2-label line is 37 +-24 BEHIND both result-label lines and
92 behind d4mix, after being 48 ahead at ply 2; d4mix's recipe plays its own games at depth 4 (with 50/50 depth-2
labels), so its ply-3 edge is the deeper GAMES. selfplay-depth cold: at ply 3 the order is d4mix 113, lrs 36, d2 0,
d2#2 -37, d0 -52, d1 -152, d4 (data-starved at 70x the cost) -376. probit-widen-p1b (pure ply-1 probit field, fresh
gate, limit 4): final versions, tree minus F-P at ply 1 / ply 2: widening 0: 104 +-42 / -21; 0.03: 81 / 36; 0.1:
-2 / -10; champions 90 / 33, 95 / 91, -4 / -27; the two F-P seeds differ by 13 / -37 (+-60). Under near-argmax play
the fresh-gate probit trees beat flat probit by 80-100 at the ply they trained at: the first routing gain that
survives T 0.02. cold-trees' probit trees (-31 at ply 1) were ply-2-trained, limit 1, stream-gated.

(a) Routing: reopened, narrowly. What differs between p1b and cold-trees is three things at once: the fresh gate
    (the trees actually held 7-9 leaves; cold-trees' were collapsed or 3-leaf), training ply = rating ply
    (specialisation, section 1), and a pure single-kind field. The reading that fits everything, including 6.9's
    theorem: a routed tree's gain is an on-distribution evaluator gain. At ply 1 the search scores only the
    children of game positions (on-distribution), so the gain shows in full (80-100 here, the ply-1 cold LR hint of
    18 in cold-trees); at ply 2 most scored positions are off-game and fragmentation costs, so the same models show
    less (p1b's trees at ply 2: -21 to 91); at ply 3 it should shrink further. So routing is a shallow-search lever
    unless a tree trained at ply 2 with a fresh gate beats flat at ply 2 cold. That is the decisive test, and it is
    p1b with ONE factor changed, the ply:
    plans/probit-trees-p2-cold.json: F-P x2 against T-P-Nc-merge limit 4, threshold 1M, --revise-on fresh,
      --split-noise 0.03 (p1b's best arm) x2, pure ply-2 field (entry_ply 2, ply1_entries false), self_games, rated
      at T 0.02 in the tournament, 12 rounds a cycle, scaled plateau min 200. Prediction under the reading above:
      30-60 (less than p1b's 80-100); under "the gate was all": 80-100.
    plans/lr-trees-p2-cold.json: the LR twin (F-L x2 against T-L-Nc-merge limit 4 fresh x2), because LR is the leaf
      for the goal (probit -177 at ply 3, 2.4x the cost). Prediction: smaller than probit's (LR leaves have no
      shrinkage), 0-40.
    Both read by a final cold arena at ply 2 and 3 of their champions. Widening 0.1 is closed (loses at both
    plies); 0.03 against 0 is within noise at ply 1 and better at ply 2, keep 0.03.
(b) The deeper-games lever: the ply-3 field, not depth-3/4 self-play lines in a ply-2 field. Reasons: at equal CPU
    the depth-4 self-play line was data-starved (-376), while d4mix won because it trained mostly on league games
    AND some deep own games; a ply-3 field gives every line ply-3 league games of a strong field (lever 1) at ~7x a
    ply-2 game, which the arenas price at roughly 0.08 CPU-s a game: 4 lines, 15 rounds a cycle, 180 games, about
    15 CPU-s of play plus training, ~30-40 CPU-s a cycle, ~3 CPU-h for 300 cycles. It needs entry_ply 3 in the
    driver (byte-identical for existing configs); worth building tonight.
    plans/flat-p3-cold.json: F-L x4 (four seeds, no other lines: the field's strength is the lever, and the
      comparator is outside the run), entry_ply 3, self_games, rated at T 0.02 in the tournament, 15 rounds a
      cycle, scaled plateau min 200. Verdict: a final cold arena at ply 3 (and 2) of its champions against
      lr-settings-new's F-L 0.0125 (ply-2 field) and d4mix v426. Prediction: the ply-3-trained F-L beats the
      ply-2-field F-L at ply 3 by 50-150 and d4mix by less or not at all (d4mix's games are deeper still).

6.11 addendum (03:35): small-field-x4-p1 cold (T-L-Tc-merge limit 1, stream gate, LR leaves, pure ply-1 field):
tree minus flat -7 +-30 at ply 1, 8 +-31 at ply 2; per tree 11, -15, -10, -13. So at ply 1 cold, LR 3-leaf
stream-gated trees gain nothing where probit 9-leaf fresh-gated trees gain 80-100. Four differences: leaf kind, gate,
leaf count, router (TAN against NB: the same partition, 6.6 c, so not that). The theory points at two: the gate and
limit (3 leaves held against 7-9: p1b's trees had real growth, small-field's a single phase split that 6.6 c showed
gains nothing at ply 1) and the leaf kind (a probit child's prior is its parent's posterior, so its leaves
specialise with shrinkage, while LR copies specialise with the parent's full step size). A mixed LR/probit field
would confound this (cluster-error: the two leaf kinds reacted oppositely to one shared stream), so the factorial
is one new cheap ply-1 run beside p1b:
  plans/lr-trees-p1-cold.json: F-L x2 against T-L-Nc-merge limit 4, threshold 1M, --revise-on fresh x2, pure ply-1
  field, self_games, rated at T 0.02 in the tournament, 12 rounds a cycle, scaled plateau min 200 (~10-15 CPU-s a
  cycle). Read cold at ply 1 and 2 at the end. If the LR fresh-gate trees gain 60-100 at ply 1: the gate and growth
  were the factor, the stream gate hid routing everywhere, and lr-trees-p2-cold becomes the ply-2 test for LR. If
  they gain nothing: the leaf kind is the factor (shrinkage makes routing work), lr-trees-p2-cold is moot, and the
  ply-2 question is probit-trees-p2-cold's alone, with the leaf-kind cost (2.4x, -177 at ply 3) weighed against it.
Priority, revised: 1. flat-p3-cold (when entry_ply 3 exists); 2. probit-trees-p2-cold; 3. lr-trees-p1-cold (in
place of lr-trees-p2-cold, which waits for 3's verdict).

6.13 (01:37, 11-11). lr-trees-p1 cold: LR fresh-gate limit-4 trees 43 +-21 above F-L at ply 1 and 43 +-22 BELOW it at ply 2 (both trained at ply 1); seed spreads 6-21. EVIDENCE for 6.9/6.11's reading: a routed tree gains at the ply it trains at (on-distribution) and loses one ply deeper, where the search scores positions its leaves never see; the sign flip between plies is the same dip seen in residual-leaves at ply 2. 6.14 (02:07, 11-11). p1c replicates p1b at ply 1 exactly (all eight widening-0/0.03 trees 84-104 above
F-P, +-26 each); at ply 2 widening 0.03 carries in all four lines (17-55, ~40), widening 0 flips between seeds
(-36/-42 and 20/8) and 0.1 is bad (-7/-39 and -149/-144). Leaf counts at the final versions (model files) and merges
(logs), p1b then p1c: widening 0: 9 and 9 leaves, 4-9 merges; 0.03: 3 and 5 leaves (18, 17 merges), then 7 and 9
(18, 15); 0.1: 1 and 1 (18 merges), then 3 and 3 (23).
(a) Mechanism. It is the split choices, through the fresh gate's selection, not the estimate's noise (the counts are
    facts, the ply-2 contrasts are 2 SE apart at most). For a probit leaf the widening IS the hierarchical prior's
    width around the parent: the posterior variance is the step size, so widening 0 leaves the children nearly as
    rigid as a 1M-example parent (they hold their nine leaves because the fresh test never sees them lose, and gain
    at ply 1 from the plasticity that splitting the stream restores), 0.1 resets every weight, rare ones included,
    to SD 0.1 (the children specialise fast and noisily, the fresh test rejects most splits, 18-23 merges, and the
    model spends its life as a freshly widened near-flat whose rare-feature weights are wide: the off-game positions
    a ply-2 search scores punish exactly that, -147), and 0.03 tethers rare features to within 0.03 of the parent
    while common ones move: the leaves that pass the fresh test are few and well tested, and they carry to ply 2.
    THEOREM (the width's two sides): for a feature with n_k examples in the leaf's region and prior SD s around the
    parent, the posterior SD is about min(s, 1/sqrt(n_k)); off-distribution error grows with s on the features
    with small n_k, on-distribution specialisation needs s above 1/sqrt(n_k) for the common ones; the right s lies
    between, and 0.03 is near it for 1M-example regions (1/sqrt(300k) = 0.002 for common features, 0.03-0.3 for
    features seen a thousand times or fewer). This is S1 done right: the LR residual sum failed because it was two
    models stepped apart (6.1); the probit copy with widening is one model with a parent-centred prior. The LR
    counterpart is a copy-on-split with an L2 pull to the parent's weights AT THE SPLIT (a frozen anchor, not the
    live shadow), width-equivalent 0.03; it needs a flag (delegation.rs: anchor per leaf, gradient += (w - a)/s^2),
    and should be swept offline on the l2sweep testbed first, then at ply 1 against lr-trees-p1's 43.
(b) Ply-3 prediction for probit-trees-p2 (widening 0.03, trained at ply 2): at ply 2 (its own) 60-90; at ply 1
    about 40 (carry towards the shallower ply has been positive); at ply 3 between 0 and 40, smaller than its ply-2
    gain, because the off-distribution share grows again but the tether holds the rare weights. A clear positive at
    ply 3 (over 40) would reopen routing FOR THE GOAL with probit leaves and the 0.03 prior, to be weighed against
    probit's own -177 at ply 3 against LR; a zero or negative would make routing a shallow lever for every leaf.
    A refinement worth queuing at ply 1 (cheap, after p1c): widening 0.01 and 0.05 beside 0.03 to find the optimum.

split's children on fresh ply-2 games (with probit leaves and widening 0.03 they did not beat the shadow inside a

mechanism (a) stands, with the addition that at ply 2 the on-distribution gain of the split is too small for the
fresh test to accept it, as 6.9 says. A change that would hold the splits (--revise 2, or a longer window) would

The free slot: (i), a second flat-p3 seed, identical but for the seed (the run-to-run spread of 50-90 is the known
hazard, and the plateau stop will leave few versions; a replicate guards the ply-3 verdict at ~3 CPU-h). Not (ii):
depth-4 self-play lines were data-starved at equal CPU (-376 at ply 3), d4mix's edge came with a strong league's
games beside its deep own games, and the ply-3 field is the cheaper way to give every line deep games of a strong
field; (ii) becomes interesting only if flat-p3 beats flat-p2 at ply 3, as the next rung (a ply-4 field or depth-4
self-games inside a ply-3 field). No better candidate aimed at depth exists without code.

6.16 (02:45, 11-11). flat-p3 cold (T 0.02): its four ply-3-trained F-L are 169-174 ABOVE the ply-2-field
F-L at ply 3 and 221-224 below it at ply 2; d4mix sits between at both plies (98 above the ply-2 models at ply 3,
123 below them at ply 2). Prediction for the ply-4 read, with the mechanism:
- THEOREM (no parity for a linear evaluator at a fixed depth): every leaf of a fixed-depth search has the same side
  to move, so the side-to-move weight is one constant added to every leaf's score and cancels in the comparison of
  moves. A linear model over one-hot squares has no other way to be parity-specific (no interaction with the side
  to move). Parity could enter only through positions scored at unequal depths (game-ending moves valued exactly at
  the first ply), a small effect. So I predict AGAINST parity.
- EVIDENCE that the training ply shapes the weights all the same: the side-to-move bonus (W-B log-odds, white to
  move minus black to move) is 0.187-0.188 in all four flat-p3 models and 0.105 in all four flat-p2 models
  (lr-settings-new 0.116-0.127): the tempo is worth nearly twice as much in ply-3 games. A diagnostic of what the
  games contain, not a cause of play differences (it cancels at a fixed depth).
- d4mix is not a clean depth-4 model: it trained 93% on the old league's games (ply-2 entries) and 7% on its own
  depth-4 games, so it is a d2/d4 mixture; that is why it sits between d2 and d3 at both plies. No parity needed.
- Prediction at ply 4: flat-p3 (distance 1) above the ply-2 models (distance 2) by 80-200, and d4mix within +-60 of
  flat-p3 (its few depth-4 games count for more at ply 4); the ordering d3 >= d4mix > d2. If instead the ply-2
  models beat flat-p3 at ply 4, the cause must be in terminal-position handling at odd against even depth (a
  search-side matter, fixed by extending captures or mates at the horizon), not in the evaluator's weights.
Next rung in each case:
- Deeper wins (predicted): "train at the deepest affordable ply", and the rung is a ply-4 field: F-L x4, entry_ply
  4, 15 rounds a cycle; cost about 5-7x a ply-3 game (hybrid10 orders captures at the last level), roughly 0.5 CPU-s
  a game, 180 games a cycle, ~2 CPU-min a cycle with training, 200 cycles ~7 CPU-h: one job. Read cold at plies
  2-5 against flat-p3's finals. The global league's one-ply-per-model rule then means one ply per model's TRAINING
  games too, as deep as the budget allows; mixed-depth fields are the old polluted field (-542) and are not the
  answer.
- Parity (not predicted): the target depth itself must be trained, and the evaluator must be made parity-free by
  the search (capture extension at the horizon); a ply-4 field would still be the test, read at plies 3 and 4.
Either way the next lever after depth is the field's strength (6.10, lever 1b), which the thunderdome's margin
rule provides, and the next model lever is features (pairs), since a linear evaluator cannot exploit depth beyond
what its weights can express.

6.17 (03:04, 11-11). Ply-4 read: flat-p3 - flat-p2 100 +-18 at ply 4, flat-p3 - lrs 57, d4mix - flat-p3
15 +-28: deeper training wins one ply further, no parity, as 6.16 predicted. Cost correction: a ply-4 flat game is
0.51 CPU-s (ply 3 0.059, ply 2 0.007), so flat-p3's recipe at ply 4 (840 games a cycle) is ~430 CPU-s a cycle and
~24 CPU-h for 200 cycles.
The cheapest decisive design for the ply-4 rung: WARM-STARTED TWINS, not a from-scratch field.
- W4: a ply-4 field whose four lines start from flat-p3's four final models (v199) and train on their ply-4 games;
  W3: the same four starts in a ply-3 field. Both 6 rounds a cycle, scaled plateau with min 60 cycles, cap 100.
  The question of the rung is "is ply-4 data better than ply-3 data for ply-4 play", and a warm start asks exactly
  that: the models already hold the evaluator, only the specialisation shift has to be learned, so 60-100 cycles
  suffice where a from-scratch field needs 200. One factor against W3: the field's ply (same starts, same cycles,
  same rounds); flat-p3's finals themselves, rated beside, show what the extra training is worth regardless of
  ply. Read cold at plies 3 and 4 (and 2). Prediction: W4 - W3 at ply 4 between 50 and 100 (one rung was worth 100
  at ply 4 and 174 at ply 3 from scratch), W3 - flat-p3 finals near 0.
- Cost: W4 at 6 rounds a cycle is 6/15 of 430 CPU-s, ~170 CPU-s a cycle, 60-100 cycles 3-5 CPU-h; W3 is ~0.5 CPU-h;
  hybrid11 would cut W4 further. Against ~24 CPU-h for the full twin.
- Needs one driver option: an initial model per line (the train command has --init/--start; the driver would copy
  flat-p3's v199 as each line's version 0, keeping the line's own seed). One caveat and its fix: a model with
  AdaGrad sums from 20M examples moves at rate/sqrt(G) and may not re-specialise on 2M new positions; apply the
  same forgetting (sums divided by 4-16, the LR forget option) to BOTH arms at the warm start, so it stays one
  factor; if the driver cannot forget, read W3's movement first (if W3 is indistinguishable from flat-p3's finals
  at ply 3, the sums froze it, and the twin needs forgetting).
- Why not the cheaper from-scratch variants: 2 lines or 6 rounds change the field's size and data per cycle, which
  are levers in their own right (6.10, lever 1), so flat-p3 is no longer the comparator; and from scratch needs the
  200 cycles anyway. If no driver option is wanted, the honest cheap variant is the full recipe at ply 4 with 6
  rounds a cycle and the twin flat-p3 RERUN at 6 rounds beside it (~10 + 1 CPU-h), the morning's decision.

6.17 note (03:06): start_forget 8 in both arms is fine (steps x2.8; the kept history is about 2M examples' worth against 3-4M new positions in 100 cycles of 6 rounds, so a consistent shift of 0.1 in a common weight's gradient moves it by rate x 0.1 x sqrt(N) ~ 5 logits-worth of headroom: not frozen). 6.18 (03:50, 11-11). Verdict of the twins (pooled, +-10): ply-3 fields are 185 below ply-2 fields at ply 2,
177 above at ply 3, ~104 above at ply 4; run to run +-7-33. What a ply-3 field teaches (weights of the 8 ply-3-field
finals against the 8 ply-2-field finals; W-B log-odds per piece, mean over its 64 squares = the material part):
  piece   ply-3  ply-2        piece   ply-3  ply-2
  P       0.29   0.22         Q       3.04   3.22
  N       0.74   0.42         K       0.04   0.32
  B       0.91   0.59         (black pieces mirror these within 0.05)
  R       1.67   1.24
Material ratios to the queen: ply-3 N 0.24, B 0.30, R 0.55; ply-2 N 0.13, B 0.18, R 0.39; classical 0.33, 0.33, 0.56.
The square tables (SD over squares) are the same size in both (0.2-0.35) and correlate 0.7-0.9 for P, N, R, Q and only
0.35-0.4 for K and B; the whole W-B table differs by a norm of 10 on a norm of 42. So the ply-3 field teaches the
VALUE OF A PIECE: minor pieces and rooks are worth 1.5-1.8x more, relative to the queen, in ply-3 games, where a
piece up is converted and pieces are not dropped back, and the ratios move most of the way to the classical ones.
THEOREM-level reading: a learned evaluator estimates P(outcome | position, play of depth k), the game-theoretic
value seen through depth-k play; it is not a property of the position alone. At depth 2 a knight up is converted
less often, so its Bayes-optimal weight IS lower there; the ply-3 model's confident material accounting then makes
wrong trades for a ply-2 player (it enters exchanges and sacrifices that ply-2 play cannot resolve), which is why
the loss at the shallower ply (185) exceeds the gain at the deeper one (104): deeper evaluators are nearer the true
values, and shallow play punishes true values. Cheap confirmation if wanted: the same table for W4's finals
against flat-p3's (the ratios should move further towards classical).
(2) The practical recipe for alpha-beta at depth D with iterative deepening. The evaluator is applied at the
horizon of the final iteration (the shallower iterations only order moves), so train at D, the deepest affordable
ply, by the ladder: from scratch at ply 2 (cheap), then warm-started rungs 3, 4, ... each from the previous rung's
finals with forgetting (W3/W4 is the first rung), since a warm rung needs about a third of the cycles. One model
per target depth is right in principle (the twins say a model is specific to its depth within about one ply, and
the loss below its depth is larger than the gain above); in practice one model, trained at D, serves every
iteration because move ordering tolerates a one-ply mismatch. Cost: a game costs 8.5x per ply here (0.007, 0.059,
0.51 CPU-s at plies 2, 3, 4; alpha-beta with good ordering should bring the factor towards 5-6, hybrid11's job),
so a rung at depth D costs about 8.5^(D-2) x the ply-2 rung's games; at 336 games a cycle and 100 warm cycles that
is ~5 CPU-h at ply 4, ~40 at ply 5, ~340 at ply 6 on this machine. Two things cut it: fewer, stronger games (the
field's strength lever, 6.10) and the data efficiency of deep games (matched-depth: 1.65M depth-4 positions beat
7.4M depth-1 ones), so the positions a rung needs should fall as D rises; and the warm start means only the shift
is learned. Ply 5 is the last affordable rung here without a faster search; beyond it the lever is the search
itself (ordering, transposition reuse), not the evaluator.

6.18 note (03:56): the read plan for W4/W3/flat-p2-w3 is right; add three contrasts from the same arena: flat-p2-w3 minus flat-p2 finals at ply 2 (the cost of climbing a rung: predicted a loss of 100-185, the shift towards true piece values that shallow play punishes); W4 minus flat-p3 finals at ply 3 (the same cost one rung up: predicted 50-100 below); and the piece-value table for flat-p2-w3 against flat-p3 (the ladder should reproduce the ply-3 values, N/B/R to Q near 0.25/0.30/0.55, if the warm start learned the rung rather than only drifting). Predictions for the headline contrasts: W4 - W3 at ply 4 between 50 and 100; W3 - flat-p3 finals within 30 at ply 3 (not frozen, nothing to learn); flat-p2-w3 - flat-p3 finals at ply 3 between -60 and 0 (a rung learned warm in 60-100 cycles reaches most of the from-scratch level).

6.19 (04:58, 11-11). Warm reads: W4 - W3 172 / -1 / 200 at plies 2 / 3 / 4; W4 - flat-p3 finals 126 /
-51 / 173; W3 - its start -46 / -51 / -27; ladder (ply-2 finals warmed into a ply-3 field) - flat-p3 finals -9 / -11,
its ply-2 cost -195. Predictions: the rung (50-100) was too timid (200); the ladder's cost and level were right;
"W3 within 30 of its start" was wrong (-27 to -51).
(1) Parity. W4 beats W3 at the even plies 2 and 4 by 170-200 and not at ply 3, and the from-scratch ply-2 models
    lost to the ply-3 ones at ply 4 by 100: both depth distance and parity act. The theorem of 6.16 stands (the
    side-to-move weight cancels at a fixed depth), so the parity must come from the DATA, not from a weight that
    reads the side to move. Mechanism (CONJECTURE): the positions of a ply-k game are selected by a ply-k search,
    whose horizon has one parity; what reaches a game is what looked best at a horizon where the side to move is
    fixed (after the opponent's reply at even k, after the mover's own move at odd k), so the training positions
    carry the systematic errors of that horizon, and the evaluator fitted to their outcomes learns to correct the
    even-horizon errors (W4) or the odd-horizon ones (W3). An evaluator used at a horizon of the same parity has
    its errors corrected; at the other parity it has the wrong corrections. A linear model can carry this because
    the corrections live in the piece and square weights (which positions-after-a-reply tend to overrate), not in
    the tempo term. Cheap decisive test (~0.5 CPU-h on hybrid11): the same W4 and W3 finals at PLY 5 and PLY 1, a few
    rounds. Parity predicts W4 at or below W3 at both odd plies (as at ply 3), depth predicts W4 above W3 at ply 5.
    If parity holds, the ladder should climb in steps of two (2 -> 4 -> 6) for an even target, and an engine with
    iterative deepening should use the evaluator only at the parity it was trained at (or extend odd horizons by a
    capture search, which is what engines do about the odd-even effect).
(2) W3's loss against its own start (27-51 at every ply). Candidates: the reheated step (forget 8: x2.8 steps on a
    model at its optimum add noise, cost ~30-50 by the measured version noise), the 6-round cycles (no effect on
    the final weights, only on how often the plateau looks) and the early stop (the noise had not decayed). The
    reheating is the likely one: W3 had nothing new to learn, so every step was noise. Cheapest decisive test: W3
    again with start_forget 1 (no reheating), everything else equal, read against flat-p3's finals (~0.6 CPU-h): if
    it holds its start within 15, the loss was the step, and W4's 173 over flat-p3 was reached despite ~40 of
    noise cost (so forget 2-4 may be the better rung setting); if it still loses, the cause is the warm field.
(3) Tonight, within 2-3 CPU-h: (i) the parity arena of (1), ~0.5 CPU-h; (ii) W3 with forget 1, ~0.6 CPU-h; then
    nothing. For the morning: the ply-5 rung decision depends on (i): if parity, the next even rung is ply 6 (far
    too costly: ~8x ply 5) and the practical target is ply 4 with a capture extension at odd horizons; if depth, a
    warm ply-5 rung from W4's finals (~30-40 CPU-h on hybrid11) is the user's call.

6.20 (05:20, 11-11). Reconciling parity with flat-p3 beating flat-p2 by 100 at ply 4.
Facts: W4 - W3 at plies 1-5: -85, 172, -1, 200, 3 (same start, same warm cycles, only the field's ply differs);
flat-p3 - flat-p2 (from scratch, 200 cycles each): -185, 177, 100 at plies 2-4; W3 with forget 1 holds its start
(7 / 22), with forget 8 loses it (-49 / -14): the reheating was noise.
Two components, learned at different speeds. (A) PARITY: an evaluator trained on ply-k games corrects the errors
of a horizon of k's parity, worth 170-200 at plies of that parity against the other parity's evaluator, and nothing
at the other parity (W4 = W3 at plies 3 and 5). It is learned FAST: 80-100 warm cycles on 3-4M positions gave W4
its full 200 over W3. (B) DEPTH: piece values and tables move towards the true ones with the depth of the games
(6.18), worth about 100 per ply at plies 3-4, at any parity; it is learned SLOWLY, from the volume of deep games
(flat-p3's 200 cycles of 840 games); a warm rung of 3-4M positions moves it only part of the way (W4's piece values
moved, R/Q 0.65, but W4 shows no depth gain over W3 at the odd plies). So at ply 4 the from-scratch ply-3 model
beats the from-scratch ply-2 model because its depth component (one ply deeper, 200 cycles) is worth more than the
ply-2 model's parity match (100 = depth 177-ish minus parity ~80 at that distance); and W4 beats W3 at ply 4 by the
parity component alone (200), having had no time to add depth. At ply 1 W4 loses 85: parity against it and two
plies of distance.
Implications and the decision. For alpha-beta with iterative deepening the horizon parity is not fixed, so a
parity-specific evaluator throws away 170-200 at half the iterations; engines remove the odd-even effect with a
capture search at the horizon (resolve pending captures before evaluating), which makes the leaf's side to move
irrelevant and should make the parity component vanish, leaving the depth component to accumulate rung by rung.
That is a search change, not a knowledge change (captures are properties of legal moves), and it is the cheapest
route to the goal: without it, every rung buys parity for one parity only; with it, every rung buys depth for all.
Recommendation: no ply-5 rung now (30-40 CPU-h for an odd-parity evaluator that W4 already matches at ply 5, 3
+-38, and ply 6 is ~8x more). First the capture extension at the horizon, then this cheapest separating test, an
arena only: the existing W4, W3, flat-p3 and flat-p2 finals at plies 2-5 with the extension on, T 0.02 (minutes to
an hour; leaves cost 2-3x). If the parity row (-85, 172, -1, 200, 3) flattens towards the depth ordering (W4 above
W3 at every ply by a steady amount, flat-p3 above flat-p2 at ply 2 as well), parity was the horizon's and the recipe
is "capture extension + train at the deepest affordable ply, warm, forget 1-2, then one rung per budget"; if the
row stays, parity is in the data itself and the target must be an even depth, ply 4 with W4, with the capture
extension only as a play-time guard. Either way the next TRAINING run is a warm rung at ply 4 from W4 with the
extension in its games (forget 2), read against W4 at plies 2-5, about 4-5 CPU-h.

6.21 (06:50, 11-11). Before the user wakes: run (a), the reverse rung, as a test of a DATA-side cure for
parity, which needs no search change.
Design: W4's four finals warm-started into a ply-3 field with start_forget 1 (forget 8 was shown to be pure noise
on a model with nothing new to learn, 6.19-6.20), 6 rounds a cycle, scaled plateau min 60, cap 100, seed 25.
Comparator: W3f1 (flat-p3's finals warmed at ply 3 with forget 1, already run), so the one factor is the START
(W4's finals against flat-p3's finals), everything else equal. Read cold at plies 1-5 (ply 5 a few rounds) against
W3f1, W4 and flat-p3's finals. ~1.4 CPU-h.
What it asks. (1) Is parity knowledge overwritten or retained? Under the model of 6.20 the parity component is
learned fast and lives in the piece and square weights, so ply-3 games should overwrite W4's even-parity
corrections within the run. (2) Does anything of W4's rung carry (its small depth component, 6.18's piece values)?
Predictions: at ply 3, the reverse rung within 20 of W3f1 (parity re-learned, no depth to add); at ply 4, it keeps
0-60 of W4's 200 over W3 (mostly overwritten; a residue if some corrections are shared); at ply 2, likewise 0-60 of
the 172; at ply 1 near W3f1. If the residue at the even plies is 60 or more, the corrections for the two parities
can COEXIST in one linear model, and the data-side cure is alternating rungs (W4 -> 3 -> 4 -> 3 ...) or a field
with both plies: a parity-neutral evaluator by data alone, worth testing next as a warm rung from the reverse rung
back at ply 4 (another ~4 CPU-h, the user's call). If the residue is near zero, one linear model cannot hold both
parities, and only the search can remove the effect: that settles the extension question with evidence rather than
on principle, which is worth the 1.4 CPU-h by itself.
(b) is free and worth doing beside it: the cross-ply Bradley-Terry (the value of one more ply per evaluator); W4's
and W3's tempo weights are in 6.19's data (-0.263 against -0.205, both above flat-p3's -0.188: the tempo rises with
the depth of the games, at both parities, so it is not the parity carrier, as the theorem says); a pending-recapture
statistic needs saved pools, which the warm runs no longer have. (c) is over budget; a seed-23 rung waits.

6.21 note (06:55): launched as flat-p3-w4-w3f1 with seed 24 (better than 25: W3f1, W3 and W4 all used 24, so the start is the only difference) and without a ply-5 read (not needed: the predictions are at plies 1-4). Free (b): one more ply, 2 to 3, is worth 641 +-28 to W3f1 and 626 +-27 to flat-p3's finals, the same evaluator searched one ply deeper; a parity-free rung of training (177) is about a quarter of a ply of search, and the parity component (200) about a third.

6.22 (07:23, 11-11). Reverse rung (W4's finals warmed at ply 3, forget 1, 97 cycles) against W3f1 (same
start recipe from flat-p3, the one factor being the start): 4 / 45 / -2 / 19 at plies 1-4 (+-15); W4 - W3f1 -96 /
131 / -87 / 131; residue of W4's even-parity gain after a ply-3 rung 0.34 at ply 2, 0.15 at ply 4. All four 6.21
predictions hold, the even residue is below the 60 threshold.
What it says. (1) One linear model holds the two parities' corrections mostly on the SAME weights: if they lived on
disjoint weights the residue would be near 1; at 0.15-0.34 a rung at the other parity overwrites most of them.
THEOREM-level consequence for a data-side cure: training on both parities (a two-ply field, or alternating rungs)
can only average the two corrections, giving roughly half the parity gain at each parity (about 65 at both instead
of 131 at one and 0 at the other). The mean over parities is the same; for a search whose final depth is fixed,
the single-parity model trained at that parity is strictly better; for iterative deepening the averaged model only
trades variance for level. So the data-side cure buys nothing at a fixed target depth, and the parity corrections
are what they look like: a patch, in the evaluator's weights, for the horizon's side-to-move error. (2) The capture
extension removes the cause rather than patching it, so it should (CONJECTURE) raise the level at BOTH parities
by freeing the weights from the patch, and it is cheaper than a ply: leaves cost 2-3x against 8x for a full ply,
while the parity component is 130-200 and a full ply of search is worth 500-700 (same-evaluator cross-ply reads).
That is the case for the user to weigh against "search last resort": the extension is the one search change that
makes every training rung count at every depth. (3) Two side findings: W4 (forget 8) sits 131 over W3f1 where it
sat 172-200 over W3 (forget 8), so the reheating cost each warm arm 40-70; rungs should use forget 1-2. And
W3f1 - flat-p3 finals is 50 +-16 at ply 4 against 9-17 at plies 2-3: another 100 cycles of ply-3 games keep adding
depth component (6.20 B) that shows one ply DEEPER before it shows at the training ply, so the plateau rule at ply
k stops a rung before it has finished learning for ply k+1; a rung's stop should be judged at the next ply, or
given a longer floor.
Next step, under the constraints (no search change without the user, lean compute, no ply-2 training):
 a. Nothing more now; the machine stays idle until the user decides on the extension. If they decline it, the
    target is an even depth and the recipe is: W4f1 (flat-p3 finals warmed at ply 4 with forget 1, ~4 CPU-h; expected
    40-70 above W4 at the even plies), then a second ply-4 rung from W4f1 for the depth component, each judged at
    ply 4 and read at ply 5 for the stop. No ply-5 rung: an odd-parity evaluator for an even target.
 b. If they accept it: the 6.20 arena first (existing finals at plies 2-5 with the extension on), then one warm rung
    at ply 4 from W4 with the extension in its games, against W4 (one factor), ~4-5 CPU-h.
 c. Cheap and free meanwhile: the per-line piece values of rev against W4 and W3f1 (did the reverse rung move the
    values back, or only the corrections?); if the piece values stayed at W4's while the parity flipped, the two
    components are separable in the weights and the parity patch is a small, identifiable set of weights, which
    would make a weight-level cure (train the patch per parity, share the rest) thinkable without a search change.
 c, done (07:23): piece values (mean W-B log-odds over squares; ratios to Q; tempo):
    flat-p3 finals  P 0.30 N 0.74 B 0.90 R 1.71 Q 3.05 | N/Q 0.24 B/Q 0.30 R/Q 0.56 | tempo 0.188
    W3f1            P 0.30 N 0.77 B 0.89 R 1.73 Q 3.08 | 0.25 0.29 0.56 | 0.191
    W4              P 0.58 N 0.97 B 1.26 R 2.24 Q 3.42 | 0.28 0.37 0.65 | 0.263
    reverse rung    P 0.29 N 0.87 B 1.11 R 1.97 Q 3.44 | 0.25 0.32 0.57 | 0.220
    ladder (p2->3)  P 0.27 N 0.69 B 0.85 R 1.53 Q 3.12 | 0.22 0.27 0.49 | 0.196
    The ply-4 rung doubled the pawn (0.30 to 0.58), raised every piece and the queen's scale, and moved the ratios
    to 0.28/0.37/0.65; the reverse rung put the pawn (0.29) and the RATIOS (0.25/0.32/0.57) back to the ply-3 values
    while keeping W4's queen scale (3.44) and part of its tempo (0.220). So the parity patch is identifiable in the
    weights: the pawn's value and the minor-and-rook-to-queen ratios flip with the parity (at an even horizon the
    mover's pending pawn pushes and captures make pawns and pieces count more against the queen), while the overall
    scale is the depth/confidence component that persists. That makes a weight-level cure thinkable without a
    search change: share the tables and learn a small per-parity part (the pawn and piece scales), i.e. a model
    with one weight vector plus a parity-indexed scale per piece type, selected by the horizon's parity at search
    time. It is a model change (a few numbers), not a search change, and it is what an evaluator for iterative
    deepening would need if the extension is declined; the test is one warm rung in a field that alternates the
    entry ply by cycle, which the driver cannot do yet. For the user's decision: the capture extension still
    removes the cause, this would only index the patch.

6.24 (08:46, 11-11). Six models for the user's next stage: candidates, growth, gaps, protocol.
First, the T 0.02 training failure (-106 at ply 1, -221 at ply 2 against T 0.1 games, floor 5-18): the mechanism is
COVERAGE, the same as 6.9's off-game theorem. Near-argmax games visit a narrow set of lines (the repetition check
by result and length misses long shared openings with late divergence), so the evaluator never sees the positions
a search scores off those lines; the loss doubles at ply 2 because the search leaves the lines further. Training
needs warm games and rating needs cold ones: two kinds of games in the global league, or warm (randomised) openings
with cold play, which gives coverage without temperature.

1. Candidates with cold ply-1 evidence, against their own leaf kind's flat (T 0.02, final versions):
   - T-P-Nc-merge, limit 4, fresh gate, widening 0 or 0.03 (NB cluster router, probit copies): 84-104 over F-P,
     eight lines in two seeded runs (p1b, p1c): REPLICATED. Widening 0.03 also carries 17-55 to ply 2, 0 does not.
   - T-L-Nc-merge, limit 4, fresh gate (LR copies): 43 +-21 over F-L, two lines, one run (lr-trees-p1); -43 at
     ply 2. UNREPLICATED (the LR-anchor fork's "without" arm will replicate it).
   - Limit-1 stream-gated trees (T-L-Tc-merge, four seeds, pure ply-1 field): -7 +-30. Closed.
   - Everything else (error trees, LR-router trees, DAGs, mixtures, FM, TAN) has NO cold ply-1 evidence in the new
     setup; the old ply-2-trained models read cold at ply 1 are not evidence about ply-1 architectures (cold-trees:
     LR cluster trees 5-36, error trees -6, probit trees -23 to -34). The pre-screen arena will rank the old
     champions cold, which closes or reopens families, but a family that survives it still needs a ply-1 run.
2. Evidence of growth. Leaf counts under the fresh gate at ply 1: p1b at v199 widening 0: 9 and 9 leaves; 0.03: 3
   and 5; p1c at v250: 9 and 9; 7 and 9; the counts rise and fall with merges (15-18 merges per 0.03 line). In
   probit-limit8-p1 at v135 (08:45) the limit-8 lines are IDENTICAL to the limit-4 lines in leaf history (1, 3, 1,
   3, 1-3 at v25-135; 1-2 merges): nothing has grown past 3 leaves, so the limit does not bind and the run cannot
   say anything about limit 8 unless it runs far past 200 cycles; the Elo is level (-7 to 7). The binding constraint
   on growth is the GATE and the window, not the limit: a second-level leaf sees a third of the stream, so its 1M
   window takes three times as long, and --revise 0 merges it the first time it fails to beat the shadow on fresh
   games. With limit 1 the trees held nothing (growth-limits-p1, stream gate) and with the fresh gate at limit 4
   they held 3-9 after 200-250 cycles. Offline (6.5) the fresh gate held 9 leaves and kept the full gain on ply-2
   data. So: growth happens, slowly, and the gain of the second level is unmeasured in play. For a growth test:
   threshold 300k (or --revise 2 so that a split is kept unless clearly worse), floor 300 cycles.
3. Gaps, cheap ply-1 runs (pure fields, self_games, plateau floor 300, read cold at plies 1-2), one factor each:
   G1. Router kind for the winner: T-P-Lc-merge limit 4 fresh 0.03 x2 (LR cluster router) against T-P-Nc-merge
       x2. The user's concern that NB routers suffer at depth as NB leaves do; LR cluster routers churned under
       the stream gate (RESULTS 3) but were never run with the fresh gate. ~1 CPU-h. Prediction: 20-40 below NB
       (the LR router's partition churns), unless the fresh gate stabilises it.
   G2. Error routing for the winner: T-P-Ne limit 4 fresh 0.03 x2 (NB error router) against T-P-Nc-merge x2.
       NB error routers route like clustering and were the old ply-2 winners at T 0.1; one factor, --route error.
       ~1 CPU-h. Prediction: within 20 of the cluster tree (same partition), so a tie closes error routing for good.
   G3. A DAG in the new setup: D-P-Nc (bdd, probit leaves, cluster routers, fresh gate, 0.03, limit 8) x2 against
       T-P-Nc-merge limit 8 x2, same leaves, one factor (router splits allowed). ~1.5 CPU-h if bdd accepts the
       flags (it has home targets P2; check --revise-on fresh and --split-noise apply to bdd). Prediction: level
       (theory 2.1-2.2 bounds the DAG's gain at 0.001-0.003 nats); its value is that the user asked for DAGs and
       no DAG has cold evidence; a tie keeps the simpler tree.
   G4. Growth proper: T-P-Nc-merge fresh 0.03, limit 8, threshold 300k x2 against threshold 1M x2 (the window as
       the factor, so that second-level splits get trials), floor 300 cycles. ~1.5 CPU-h. Prediction: 7-15 leaves

   Order with three jobs: G4 and G1 now (they use the winner's recipe), G5 when its fork lands, then G2, G3.
4. Selection protocol for the six. Two flats (F-L, F-P) plus four. "Best" means the gain over the SAME leaf kind's
   flat at ply 1, because the leaf kind is a separate lever and the user wants architectures that expand; the
   gap to the best flat overall is reported beside it (the pre-screen gives F-L against F-P cold at ply 1 of the
   same vintage), since a probit tree worth 95 over F-P is worth less if F-P is 100 behind F-L. Requirements for a
   seat: replicated in two seeds; gain of at least 40 at ply 1 (two standard errors of a 75-round read); leaves
   held and rising with data (the model files); and a ply-2 read beside, as a guard against on-distribution-only
   gains (a loss of more than 60 at ply 2 against its flat disqualifies, since the user will deepen later). The
   final arena: all candidates' final versions AND champions, both flats of the same vintage, one cold arena at
   plies 1 and 2, T 0.02, hybrid13, 75 round-robins (+-20), Bradley-Terry among entries; rank by gain over own
   flat, break ties by the ply-2 read and by leaves held. Today's seats on the evidence: T-P-Nc-merge limit 4
   fresh 0.03 (seat 1, replicated), T-L-Nc-merge limit 4 fresh (seat 2 if G5's comparator arm replicates the 43),
   and two seats open to G1-G4's winners; if none of G1-G4 beats its comparator, the remaining seats go to the
   same recipes at a second widening (0) and a second router (TAN), which are different models with the same
   evidence, and the user should know the four are then variants of one archetype.

6.24 note, G5 (09:14): drop the league run. The offline sweep says the anchor at the matched strength (L* 1.3e-3) makes the children unable to beat the shadow inside a gate window, so the fresh gate merges the tree to its root (round 33, every seed) and the tree collapses; the only strength that never merges (4e-4) does nothing. That is a mechanism, not a measurement gap: a tether and a fresh gate on the same window are at odds, and the probit widening that worked did so partly by plasticity (widening 0, the most rigid children, gained 95 at ply 1), the opposite of what an anchor does. The anchor's motivation was the ply-2 carry, which is set aside with depth. Keep chessai-hybrid6an; revisit only when depth returns, with --revise 2 or a longer window so that tethered children get their trial, L in {4e-4, 1.3e-3}, at ply 2. Seat 2's second seed comes from G6's plain fresh-gate LR arm; do not fold the anchored arm into another field (its games would enter the others' streams).

6.24 note, G3 (09:42): (a), the cell DAG D-L-Nc on dag-q9 (LR leaves, 9 cells, window 50k, dag-z 2, threshold 1M) against T-L-Nc-merge limit 4 fresh, F-L x2 beside, ply 1, floor 300. Use --dag-leaves 5, not 3: a DAG's one distinctive capacity is more cells than leaves (sharing, re-pointing), and 5 shared leaves over 9 cells gives it that while staying near the trees' 3-5 held leaves; with 3 it is a 9-cell phase router over three leaves, a tree with softer boundaries.
