6.25 (09:49, 11-11). Design: one architecture in which a router "splits" only by the loss measured to its
routing, so tree and DAG are one model (the user's idea, 09:50). Data in hand: on G4's probit trees the group-level
regret of the current B-way routing is 0.0000-0.0040 nats per position per router (per-position hindsight 0.04-0.10,
an unachievable bound), and every second-level router's children are near-duplicates (closest pairs 0.21-0.44 in mean
|log-odds difference| against an all-pairs mean of 1.4-4.8). So the data say: little is lost to routing, and more
is lost to copies that never parted. The design must contain both moves.
(1) The statistic: achievable routing regret at a finer partition, selected on one fresh window and scored on the
    next. Each router keeps CELLS, a finer partition of its positions that costs no new parameters: a cell is the
    router's own posterior read coarsely, (first child, second child, confidence bin), at most B(B-1) x 2 = 12 cells
    for B = 3 (a separate label-free NB probe with K cells is the alternative, at K x 70 x 13 counts, if the
    posterior's cells prove too coarse). During the fresh pass that --revise-on fresh already makes (the round's
    positions scored by the model at the round's start, every child's subtree loss per position, which the merge
    test uses), accumulate per cell c and child k the loss L_c^t(k) and the count n_c^t, game ids kept for the SE.
    At the window's end choose the mapping m(c) = argmin_k L_c^t(k); on the NEXT window score
      G^{t+1} = sum_c [ L_c^{t+1}(h(c)) - L_c^{t+1}(m(c)) ] / n^{t+1},   h = the current mapping (identity at first),
    with a standard error clustered by game (as T4's test). G is the loss the current routing loses to the best
    mapping of these cells to these children, measured out of sample: the "amount being lost to routing". The
    bddregret defect (fresh copies whose regret starts at zero and can only rise) does not arise: nothing is copied,
    and G is a level, not a trend. The same pass yields the reverse statistic: for children j, k the fresh loss of
    each on the other's positions, D_jk = [L(j on k's positions) - L(k on k's positions)] and vice versa; children
    with both D within 2 SE of zero are duplicates.
(2) What a router split creates: a cell layer, not new leaves. The router keeps its classifier; its routing becomes
    cell -> child through the mapping m, so several cells may point to one child (sharing) and a child may receive
    cells the argmax would not have sent (re-pointing): the cell DAG's operation inside a tree, with the tree as the
    identity mapping. The reverse move, leaf sharing: two duplicate children are merged into one leaf (the one with
    more data; the other's cells re-pointed to it), which pools their data and frees the split budget; the freed leaf
    is deleted, not kept. Both moves change only the mapping table (K small integers per router) and the leaf set.
(3) The gate: adopt a new mapping only when G^{t+1} > z SE with z = 2 on the next window, and keep it only while the
    following windows' G of the identity against it stays below z SE (the same fresh test in reverse undoes it).
    Charging parameters: a mapping adds K log2(B) bits and nothing to the leaves; charge it as a description-length
    term, G must exceed z SE + K ln B / n^{t+1} (negligible at 1M, principled, and it answers the user's concern:
    there are no path parameters, only a table, and the table is paid for by measured gain). If the probe variant is
    used, its counts are label-free side state, trained like T4's probe and dropped at the split limit.
(4) Identity: with the mapping at identity the routing is the argmax, so training and play are today's BDT; the cell
    accumulators are side sums over the fresh pass that already runs, touching no RNG, order or weight; a run with
    the option whose gate never fires is byte-identical to one without it, which is the test (T4 and the target
    build passed the same kind). Model files gain a mapping block only when a mapping is non-identity.
(5) Linear in size: per router K x B loss sums and K integers; per example one cell lookup on a posterior already
    computed; no leaf multiplies at a router split, and sharing reduces leaves. Leaf splits grow the experts as now;
    cell mappings grow the routing without fragmenting data: the decoupling of 2.5, with the DAG's parameters zero
    until paid for.
(6) Prediction and the cheapest test. The routing move is bounded by the headroom measured every way so far: at the
    current partition 0.000-0.004 nats, at finer partitions the ridge router and re-pointing studies found at most
    0.005; at ply 1 that is 0-20 Elo, i.e. the mapping gate will rarely fire and the model will mostly BE the tree,
    which is the user's requirement. The sharing move is the one the data ask for: G4's near-duplicate pairs waste a
    split each; merging them pools 2-3M positions per pair and returns the budget, worth about d/(2n) per pair in
    loss (small) and, in play, whatever the freed splits earn elsewhere. Honest effect size at ply 1: within 20 of
    the tree, with fewer wasted leaves. Cheapest test, no training: a small tool over the existing G4 models and
    their saved fresh rounds (the routing command already prints every child's loss per router group; it needs the
    per-position dump by cell) that computes G with the select-then-score protocol at every router: if G is under
    0.002 nats everywhere, the cell move cannot pay on these leaves and only the sharing move should be built; if
    any router shows 0.005 or more out of sample, build both and run one ply-1 line (the unified model with z 2)
    against T-P-Nc-merge limit 4 fresh, F-P x2 beside, ~1 CPU-h, read cold at plies 1-2.

6.25 addendum (09:52, 11-11).
G4 read: window 1M 51 over F-P at ply 1 (46, 57), window 300k -47 (-3 with one leaf, -91 with three); 1M minus 300k
99 at ply 1, 67 at ply 2. My sign was wrong: a shorter window does not grow the tree, it lets the fresh test's noise
decide. At 300k the test's SE is ~1.8x larger, so --revise 0 (merge at any negative gain) rejects good splits more
often (300k #1 ended flat) and, by the same noise, keeps a bad one (300k #2, three leaves, -91). THEOREM-level rule
for the gate: the window must make the fresh test's SE small against the split's real gain (0.003-0.008 nats on
these data, so a window of 1M or more at B = 3), and the merge threshold should be asymmetric (merge only when
clearly worse, --revise 2) if growth is wanted; growth is gate-limited, not limit-limited (6.24, item 2), and a
grown tree needs long runs, not short windows. Seat 1's recipe: 44 and 51 in 6-line fields, 87-104 in 8-line
fields; the field, not the recipe, sets the size.
Path cells on shared leaves (the user's "path parameters", 09:55; bddctx's design). Yes, the sharing move should
carry one: when two near-duplicate children are merged into one shared leaf, each route into it keeps a path cell,
3 numbers per route per class, 12 per sibling set at B = 3. THEOREM (5.3 and 2.6): for LR this is a fixed-effects
model, one piece-square table shared by the routes plus a per-route intercept, a nested convex class, so the
population optimum is never worse than the shared leaf without cells, and the cell is neutral at creation (zeros).
For a PROBIT leaf the cell is one extra weight in the latent mean per route, with its own posterior mean and
variance: start it at mean 0 and the widening variance (0.03^2), so that the merged leaf's prediction mean is
unchanged at creation and the intercept is learned under the same hierarchical prior as the leaves themselves; it
costs one posterior pair per route per class and nothing at play beyond an addition. Its only effect on the
predictive is a slight widening by the cell's variance, which the fresh test will see as a small loss until the
cell learns; acceptable, and the merge gate below accounts for it.
The duplicate test should then be on SLOPES only: compute D_jk after shifting child j's log-odds on k's positions by
the best per-class intercept (the mean residual on the window, one number per class, fitted on window t, applied
on t+1 like everything else). Two children whose intercept-corrected cross-losses are within 2 SE of each other's own
are duplicates up to an intercept, which the cell absorbs exactly. The merge gate is asymmetric, as parsimony wants:
merge when the shared leaf with cells is NOT clearly worse than the pair on the next window (gain above -2 SE),
since the merge removes a leaf's worth of parameters and returns a split to the budget; the reverse (a shared leaf
re-split into copies) needs the usual positive gain at 2 SE. Cells live only on shared leaves, so a tree that never
shares has none, and a tree whose routers never earn a mapping has no DAG parameters at all: the user's point holds
in both directions. CNBP leaves are excluded from sharing with cells (theory 2.6: a cell double-counts the routers'
features in a naive-Bayes leaf); the user has no NB leaves in the league, so nothing is lost.

6.25 addendum 2 (10:01, 11-11): the user's stall-triggered router split (copies under a central cluster
router R', the fresh gate disposing) against the mapping design.
- Trigger: right. A subtree's error stalls as its leaves stall (every leaf does, theory 7.2), so it proposes
  often; that is fine when the gate disposes, and it needs no G statistic.
- Expected gain: both variants are capped by the routing headroom (0.001-0.005 nats; the routecells diagnostic
  will put a number on it). The copies variant can express finer partitions (piecewise-NB, theory 5.2) but
  FRAGMENTS THE ROUTER's data (each copy clusters a third of the stream; bddstudy: copies at F = 1 agree 94-97%
  with each other for a long time, at F = 16 they part at once and route worse for 4-8 rounds); the mapping design
  fragments nothing and re-points on measured loss. Failure modes of the copies variant: (a) copies re-cluster to
  the same phase partition within their share (a finer phase band: possibly useful, possibly a no-op); (b) the
  trial's shadow must be the UNSPLIT SUBTREE trained in parallel on everything, not the delegator's leaf shadow
  of today, so a router trial doubles that subtree's training cost for a window; (c) the chain of pass-through R'
  nodes is prevented by the hash warm-up only while the copies are identical; once they part, R' learns label-free
  which copy to use, a partition over partitions whose value is unmeasured. Code: the mapping design needs the cell
  sums in the existing fresh pass and a mapping table; the copies variant needs router copies inside the BDT, a
  subtree shadow, R' with hash warm-up, forgetting on router counts and a port of the linear path cells: clearly
  more. If one is built first, the mapping design, and only after the diagnostic clears 0.005; the copies variant
  is the right second step if the diagnostic shows headroom the mapping cannot reach (G large at the finest cells).
- Priority rule: leaves first. At a stall, a leaf split is proposed if any leaf below is under its limit and has
  not failed its last fresh trial; a router split only when every leaf below is at its limit or has failed its
  last trial. One trial per subtree at a time (a window belongs to one trial), and a router trial never starts
  inside a subtree whose leaf trial is open.
- Forgetting for the copies: on the copies' ROUTER counts only (never on leaves), F = 4 as the compromise between
  F = 1 (copies never part) and 16 (they part at once and route worse); the hash warm-up breaks symmetry without a
  prediction change (copies of identical routers over shared children predict identically), so the re-routing
  cost at the split is zero and grows only as the copies drift, which the fresh gate measures. The route switch's
  -28 does not apply: that swapped a trained router for another; this adds nothing until the gate accepts.
- Error targets below a cluster R': no. NB error routers route like clustering anyway (U 0.02-0.12) and LR error
  routers are evaluators in disguise (1.3); the label's information enters through the fresh GATE, which is a
  merge, and the user's rule keeps error trees non-merging, so error copies could not be gated. Cluster throughout.

6.25 addendum 3 (10:02, 11-11): the routecells diagnostic (unified/summary.txt). Out-of-sample G at the
finest cells is -0.0014 to 0.0004 nats per position pooled, no router reaches 0.005 or 2 SE (largest 0.0040 +-0.0022),
15 of 17 readings at or below 0.001; the routing tool's in-sample regret was selection bias (0.0019 in sample,
-0.0012 out). Duplicates: every second-level router in the five G4 trees holds a pair within 2 SE (20 of 30 pairs
on slopes), root children never; one generalist leaf (4) matches its siblings on their positions while they are
0.06-0.36 worse on its. VERDICT: the routing headroom at finer partitions is zero on these leaves, so router
splits, by G or by the user's subtree-error trigger, would be merged back by any honest gate; the mapping and
copies designs are both moot until the leaves change. Sharing is the only move the data support, and it is a tidy-
up (variance pooling), not a gain: do not build it now either.
The cheaper lever: make the gate DEMAND a positive gain. Today --revise 0 keeps a split whose fresh gain is merely
not negative, which keeps zero-gain second-level splits about half the time (they are copies); a merge rule of
"merge unless the branch beats the shadow by +2 SE on the fresh window" (a negative z in today's convention, one
flag value if the code accepts it) removes them, keeps 3-5 real leaves, and returns the budget; it measures the
thing itself where a minimum-divergence rule would measure a proxy (two leaves can differ and both be wrong).
Prediction at ply 1: the same gain as seat 1 with fewer leaves and fewer merges; a wash in Elo, a cleaner model.
What would make deeper splits specialise at all. THEOREM-level: a second-level split's children are copies of a
leaf that already fits one phase; a label-free NB router partitions its share by the same phase-like statistics,
so the sub-partition is a material band inside a phase, along which a linear table has little to gain (the
first-order gain along the probe's partition was the same at every leaf, 1.2); the children see a ninth of the
stream, so a 1M window takes nine times longer and the fresh test judges them before they have moved; and the
tether (widening 0.03) holds them to the parent meanwhile. So copies stay copies by construction. Three things
would change that, in order of cost: (a) windows proportional to the leaf's share (a second-level leaf gets its
trial after 1M of ITS examples, not of the stream's), so the gate judges a child that has trained; (b) informed
partitions: choose the second-level split's partition among label-free candidate probes (phase, king zone,
pawn structure, material class) by the first-order gain T4 already computes, and initialise the children along
its per-region gradient directions (S3), so the split lies where the leaf's gradient disagrees rather than along
another phase band; (c) richer leaf features (S7): on one-hot squares the useful partition is one phase split, and
the measured headroom beyond it is zero; growth past 3-5 leaves on these features is not a gate problem but a
feature problem. (a) is a flag-sized change and should go with the positive-gain gate; (b) is the trigger-src
work parked in 5.4; (c) is the long road, and the honest reading of today's numbers is that the four seats will be
variants of one 3-5-leaf archetype until (c).

6.26 (10:05, 11-11). The user's decisions: error trees become DAGs now (no leaf sharing of any kind,
error routers non-merging, router splits permanent, calibrated by --router-split-z); cluster trees later.
(a) Arms for error-dag-p1 (pure ply-1 LR field, floor 300, read cold at plies 1-2): F-L x2; T-L-Ne limit 4,
    threshold 1M, non-merging, no sharing x2 (the tree; by the fork's identity check it equals D-L-Ne at z = inf, so
    it is the DAG's own comparator); D-L-Ne with path context at two z values x2 each. Which z: NOT by offline loss
    (see b) but by router-split COUNT in the offline rounds: the z that gives about one or two router splits per
    four leaf splits, and the z that gives about four to six, so the league sees a sparse and a busy DAG; if no
    finite z separates the counts (bddtrig: z 2 "changes nothing" because the trigger's rise is systematic and its
    per-position SE tiny), the knob is dead and the fork should cluster the SE by game (as T4 does) before the
    sweep, or add the priority rule of 6.25 (a router may split only when every leaf below is at its limit or has
    failed its trial), which is what calibrates in practice. Limit mapping: --split-limit counts LEAF splits in both
    kinds (router splits add no leaves), so limit 4 means four leaf splits in the tree and in the DAG, with router
    splits capped at the number of leaf splits made (never more routers than leaves' worth of data). Sub-router
    target: HOME (P2), with the realised-subtree tie-break of theory 9.4 if the fork has it; bddhome showed home
    keeps R' from collapsing (38/48/14 at round 60 against 42/56/2 for routing targets) and the copies part. Path
    context: in BOTH DAG arms (the user's path parameters are the point of the DAG; bddctx's full form is fine at
    limit 4, and "linear form" if the fork has it), so that z is the one factor between the DAG arms and the tree
    arm is the comparator for "any DAG at all". Prediction: the DAG arms within 20 of the tree at ply 1 (the
    out-of-sample routing headroom on these leaves is zero, 6.25 addendum 3, and bddhome's R' splits were level
    with a same-leaves tree); the error tree against F-L at ply 1 between -20 and 20 (no cold ply-1 evidence yet
    for any error tree in the new setup; the fresh-gate LR cluster tree's 43 came with the gate the error tree
    cannot have, and 1.3's lean attractor is the risk). The user should know this before the read: if the error
    family sits at or below flat at ply 1, the six will hold no error model, DAG or not, by the seat rule.
(b) Can the offline sweep pick z? Not for gain: five studies and seven variants put every router-split variant
    within +-0.0015 of a same-leaves BDT, and z 2 changed nothing in bddtrig; the sweep will show z-insensitive
    losses and can only pick the z that splits least while staying level, which is the tree. Its useful outputs
    are two: whether z moves the split COUNT at all (the knob's liveness), and whether path context costs anything
    at these sizes. Choose the league z values by count, as in (a), and treat the league read as the calibration.
(c) Cluster trees, ranked plan (from 6.25's addenda), with what each needs:
    1. Positive-gain gate: keep a split only when the branch beats its shadow by +2 SE on the fresh window (today
       --revise 0 keeps any non-negative gain and so keeps zero-gain copies half the time). FLAG-SIZED if --revise
       accepts a negative z; otherwise a few lines. Test: seat 1's recipe with it against without, ply 1, ~1 CPU-h.
       Prediction: the same gain with fewer leaves and merges.
    2. Windows in the leaf's own examples: check first whether a leaf's window already counts its own scored
       examples (split_threshold per leaf) rather than the stream's; if it does, the slow second level is inherent
       (a ninth of the stream takes nine times the cycles) and the fix is the run length, not a flag: set the
       floor in cycles so that second-level leaves get two trials. FLAG-SIZED or nothing.
    3. Informed second-level partitions: choose the split's partition among label-free candidate probes (phase,
       king zone, pawn structure, material class) by the first-order gain T4 computes, and initialise the children
       along the per-region gradient directions (S3), so that a sub-split lies where the leaf's gradient disagrees
       and not along another phase band. CODE (trigger-src has the statistic and the probe; the candidate probes
       and the initialisation are new). Prediction: second-level leaves that part, and 10-30 at ply 1 if any
       within-phase structure exists on these features.
    4. S7, richer leaf features, the only lever past 3-5 leaves on one-hot squares. Zero-knowledge version: the
       candidate set is every pair of existing one-hot features (square-value x square-value), nothing hand-made;
       the model stays a linear table over the enlarged feature list; which pairs enter is LEARNED from the data by
       the same first-order statistic (the mean gradient of a pair indicator at the current model, zero when the
       pair adds nothing), added a few at a time, zero-initialised and shrunk; pairs that stay near zero are
       dropped. It is cascade-correlation on indicator pairs, data-chosen, so it respects the constraint. CODE (a
       leaf kind with a growing feature list; this is also where sharing of pair tables across leaves would start
       to matter, 2.1). Prediction: offline gain above 0.01 nats per hundred pairs if any interaction matters;
       this is the lever the user's "chess is complex" points at.

6.28 (10:44, 11-11). G6: seat 2 (T-L-Nc-merge limit 4 fresh) is 19 +-22 over F-L at ply 1 here against
43 in lr-trees-p1, and -17 at ply 2; the LR error tree with sharing 22 / -34; all at 9 leaves. The LR family's

Prediction for error-growth-p1 (F-P x2, T-P-Ne limit 4 x2 against limit 8 x2, ply 1, floor 300, non-merging):
limit 8 within +-20 of limit 4 at ply 1 by cycle 300, more likely a little below (-10 to -30) than above, with the
limit-8 trees at 13-17 leaves of which the third-level ones are young. Reasons: a third-level leaf sees a 27th of
the stream, so its first 1M examples take ~85 cycles at ~320k positions a cycle and it is judged by play while
barely trained; non-merging keeps every split, so the fragmentation is permanent; and the routing headroom at
finer partitions measured zero out of sample (6.25 addendum 3), on cluster-trained and error-trained leaves alike
(G1's NB tree was in the diagnostic). A gain of 20 or more for limit 8 by cycle 300 would mean error splits find
within-phase competence that label-free probes do not, which would reopen 6.25's mapping design for error trees.
Does the phase-split/feature reading carry over to label-driven splits? Mostly yes: NB error routers route like
clustering (U 0.02-0.12), their sub-partitions are phase-like bands too, and the leaves are the same linear
tables, so the ceiling past 9 leaves is the features' (S7), not the trigger's. The one real difference is that an
error split can in principle be placed where the children's losses differ, which a cluster split cannot; the
routecells numbers say that on one-hot squares there is nothing there to place it on. So for the six, the
flagship's "expands in size" claim should be stated as: it expands to 9 leaves with gain, and past that only when
the leaves have more to learn from; error-growth-p1 tests exactly that at the first extra level.

6.28 correction (10:45). "Zero headroom on cluster- and error-trained leaves alike" was not measured for
error trees at the time; it is now (unified/errtrees): on G2's two T-P-Ne trees the out-of-sample G is 0.0011 and
-0.0000 per decisive position at the cells, 0.0002 at the B-way partition, largest per router 0.0019 +-0.0012; so
the re-routing headroom is about zero for error trees too, and the conclusion stands as measured. The new fact is
the duplicates: the error trees have almost none (one pair in ne1, none in ne2; most sibling pairs differ by
0.01-0.07 out of sample with SEs of 0.002-0.005), where every second-level cluster router held a near-copy pair.
So label-driven splits DO place distinct children, out of sample, and what is left to win by re-routing among
them is nothing; the gain of the partition is already in the leaves.
Does it change the error-growth-p1 prediction? Somewhat, upward. G bounds re-pointing, not splitting: a new split
adds parameters, and error splits are now shown to place real partitions at level 2, so a third-level error split
is likelier to place a real one than a cluster split would. The limits that remain are data and time (a 27th of
the stream per leaf, ~85 cycles to its first 1M examples) and permanence (no gate). Revised: limit 8 minus limit 4
between -20 and 20 at ply 1 by cycle 300, centred near 0, with a late gain of 10-30 plausible if the run goes
past 400 cycles; a loss beyond 30 would say the third level fragments faster than it learns. The useful read
beside Elo: the third-level leaves' cross-fit D_jk at the end (distinct or copies), which decides whether a
limit-16 run is worth anything.
Side note worth a line for the user: in the game-generating arenas, uncalibrated at T 0.1, T-P-Ne was 1-6 Elo
over F-P where the calibrated T 0.02 read gives 118. An evaluator's edge is in its RANKING of moves; a softmax at
T 0.1 follows the ranking loosely and on a scale set by each model's own confidence, so it dilutes a ranking edge
and adds a scale term of either sign; near argmax the ranking decides. That is why the trees' gains appeared only
when rating went cold, and why every earlier "no gain" at T 0.1 was uninformative rather than negative.

6.29 (11:18, 11-11). Prediction for error-dag-probit-p1 (F-P x2; T-P-Ne limit 4 x2; D-P-Ne-ctx, leaf
limit 4 plus router limit 4, z 0 x2 and z 30 x2; ply 1, floor 300, read cold at plies 1-2), written before any read.
Offline the DAG arms were level with the tree in full log-loss (within 0.002, z 20 worse by 0.0024) and 0.004
better in winner log-loss at z 0-10; router splits fire in a band (cliff between z 20 and 25) because the trigger's
rises are systematic, so z 30 is "the band's edge" and z 0 "always".
Prediction: both DAG arms within 20 of T-P-Ne at ply 1 and at ply 2, i.e. no seat earned by the DAG on Elo. Point
estimates: z 30 at 0 to 10 above the tree, z 0 at -15 to 5. Reasons: the re-routing headroom on these very leaves
measured zero out of sample (6.28 correction), so the central router R' and its copies can redistribute positions
among children that are already well assigned; the path cells add one latent per route, which is neutral at
creation and worth at most a per-route intercept (theory 5.3, bounded by the base-rate differences between routes,
small on decisive positions); the winner-loss gain offline (0.004) is the kind that has never carried into play
here. The structural read is the one to report: whether the copies part (R' overrule rate, as bddhome's 35-40% by
round 30) or pass through, and whether the cells move (mean |cell| at the end; near 0.03 means they never learned).
A DAG arm 25 or more above the tree in BOTH plies would be the first router-split gain ever measured in play and
would be worth a second seed; 25 or more below at z 0 would mean the busy DAG's permanent router splits fragment
the routers' data (each copy sees a third), and z 30's band is the only usable setting. The user's framing holds in
expectation: calibrated, the DAG equals the tree; the question the run answers is whether "equals" costs anything.

6.30 (11:33, 11-11). probit-confirm-p2 misses 6.27: in a ply-2 field, with 9 leaves held from v99, the
probit error tree is -17 / 18 / -49 against F-P at plies 1-3 and the --revise-2 cluster tree -3 / -9 / -65. The
same recipe in a ply-1 field was 118 / 77 at plies 1-2. So the architecture's gain depends on the FIELD it trains
in, not only on the ply it is read at.
(1) Verdict and mechanism. Candidates, with what the data say:
  - Data per leaf (the trees need more at ply 2): a ply-2 cycle here has ~82k positions against ~320k at ply 1, so
    at the stop the ply-2 trees had ~2M positions per leaf against ~11M in G2; the run's 242 cycles equal ply-1
    cycle ~60 in positions. Credible and TESTABLE WITHOUT TRAINING (below).
  - The ply-2 field's play makes the phase partition worth less: THEOREM-level candidate, from 6.18. The evaluator
    estimates P(outcome | position, play of depth k). Under shallow play outcomes are dominated by blunders whose
    rates differ by phase, so P(win | material edge) depends on phase and a phase partition corrects a lot; under
    deeper play a material edge converts in every phase, the value function is nearer a linear material count, and
    the partition has little to add. The partition's gain is a correction to the shallow-play value function. This
    predicts the gain shrinks as the FIELD's play deepens, at any reading ply, and that the ply-1-trained tree's
    gain over the ply-1 flat survives when both are read at ply 2 (it did, 77) because both carry the same
    shallow-play value function.
  - The ply-2 flat already captures what the trees capture: the same thing as the previous candidate, said from
    the flat's side.
  - Noisier routing target at ply 2: unlikely (90% decisive games give a cleaner target, and the cluster tree, with
    no label target, lost the same way).
  The two live candidates are data per leaf and the field's play. They separate on existing models: read G2's
  versions at EQUAL DATA (G2 v60-65, ~20M positions, against its own F-P at the same versions, cold, minutes). If
  the ply-1 trees already lead by 60-100 there, data is not the explanation and the field's play is; if they are
  level, probit-confirm-p2 stopped short of its data and a longer ply-2 run (or a bigger field) would show the gain.
  My expectation: the ply-1 trees lead at equal data (p1b's trees were crowned from cycle ~50), so the field's play
  is the mechanism, with data a secondary term.
(2) Dated prediction for cross-ply-probit-cold (ne1, fp1 from the ply-1 field v314; ne2, fp2 from the ply-2 field
    v241; plies 1-3): fp1 - fp2: 100 to 200 at ply 1, -300 to -450 at ply 2, -400 to -600 at ply 3 (specialisation,
    as the flat twins at plies 2-3 showed, -185 / 177). ne1 - fp2: fp1 - fp2 plus the tree's own gain, 200 to 320 at
    ply 1, -230 to -380 at ply 2, -360 to -570 at ply 3: the field's ply dominates the architecture by a factor of
    three to five at the reading plies that matter. ne2 - fp2: within 25 at every ply (the read above).
(3) For the six and the user's rule. This is the "good reason to think low-ply results do not carry": the
    flagship's 118 at ply 1 is 0 when the same architecture trains on ply-2 games, with its leaves held, and the
    cluster seat behaves the same. Ply-1 screening therefore selects architectures for the ply-1 value function,
    which by (1) is where a partition has the most to correct; it does not rank architectures for deeper play, in
    either direction (6.8 already showed ply-2-trained trees gaining nothing at any ply). The cheapest test that
    tells the user whether to keep screening at ply 1 is the equal-data read above (minutes, no training): if it
    shows the gain is the field's play, screening must move to the ply the evaluator will be used at, which for
    the user's stated aim means a ply-2 screen now (3-4x the cycle cost; a probit tree field at ply 2 ran ~1.5
    CPU-h to its plateau here) and ply 1 only for mechanism readouts; if it shows the gain is data, keep the ply-1
    screen and give the ply-2 confirmation runs the positions per leaf the ply-1 runs had (bigger fields or longer
    floors). Either way the six should be presented with both numbers, the ply-1 gain and the ply-2-field gain,
    and the honest line is that on today's evidence no tree beats its flat when trained on ply-2 games.

6.31 (11:50, 11-11; written on the v226 arena's INTERIM standings, 15 of 75 round-robins, per-player
+-34-42, so contrasts +-50; the coordinator should replace (a)'s numbers with the 75-round read).
(a) Mechanism, data against the field's play. Interim at equal data (G2 v226, 46.5M positions, the same as
    probit-confirm-p2's finals): error tree minus F-P about 19 at ply 1 and 73 at ply 2; cluster tree about 0 and
    1. The rule was "60 or more at ply 1 says the field's play". At ply 1 the interim says NO: the ply-1 trees'
    118 at v314 was not there at v226, so a part of the ply-1 gain forms late, with data and time, and the ply-2
    run (stopped at v241 by its plateau) may have stopped before its own version of it; at ply 2 the ply-1 error
    tree already leads by ~70 at v226 where the ply-2-trained tree, with the same data and its own 9 leaves,
    leads by nothing. So both terms are real: data and time account for part of the ply-1 gain, and the field's
    play for the whole of the ply-2 tie (a ply-2-trained tree has no gain to form at its own ply even at equal
    data, while a ply-1-trained one already has one there). If the 75-round read moves the ply-1 figure above 60
    the data term drops out and the field's play is the whole story; if it stays near 20, the honest statement is
    "ply-1 trees gain late, ply-2 trees do not gain at all within the same data".
(b) The ply-3 miss. The ply-1-field models lose less at ply 3 (fp1 -159, ne1 -246 against fp2) than at ply 2
    (-316, -267): PARITY, as in the W4 result. Ply 1 and ply 3 share the odd parity (the opponent to move at the
    horizon), so the ply-1-field models' parity corrections are right at ply 3 and wrong at ply 2; two components
    fit the flats exactly: a depth component D and a parity component P with -D - P = -316 at ply 2 and -D + P =
    -159 at ply 3, giving D 237 and P 79 (the same two components as 6.20, smaller P here). Why both trees sit
    below their flats at ply 3 (ne1 - fp1 -88, ne2 - fp2 -71): a tree is MORE specialised to its training field
    than its flat is. Its leaves each hold their own copy of the field's corrections learned on a third of the
    data, and a search one or two plies away from the training ply scores positions off the leaves' regions
    (6.9), so the tree's advantage decays faster with reading-ply distance than the flat's strength does; at the
    same parity two plies away (ne1 at ply 3) and at the wrong parity one ply away (ne2 at ply 3) the decay has
    already eaten the whole gain. Rule of thumb from the three reads: a tree's gain over its flat is at its own
    ply, about half of it one ply away at the training parity, and negative two plies away or at the other parity.
(c) The consequence and the plain choice. A ply-1 screen picks architectures for the ply-1 field; the same
    architecture trained at ply 2 ties its flat (ne2 - fp2: 0, -4, -71), with its leaves held and the same data. The
    user's premise, "deeper training makes any model stronger at deeper plies, so it doesn't pick an
    architecture", is half right: deeper training lifts flats and trees alike (fp2 over fp1 by 316 at ply 2), but
    it also REMOVES the trees' edge, so the ply-1 ranking of architectures does not transfer. The choice:
    (A) keep screening at ply 1, knowing the six are ply-1 architectures, which is right only if the plan is to use
        them at ply 1 or to climb from them by warm starts (and the cross arena says ply-1-field models start 250-
        320 behind ply-2-field ones at plies 2-3, so the climb begins far back);
    (B) screen at ply 2, the user's own confirmation ply, at 3-4x the cycle cost (~1.5 CPU-h for a 6-line probit
        run to its plateau), accepting that on today's architectures the ply-2 screen returns "flat", which is the
        true answer for ply 2 and the right starting point for the next architecture work (features, S7, since a
        phase partition corrects a shallow-play value function and has little to correct in a deeper one).
    Recommended next step, one run: the ply-2 screen of the two probit trees against F-P with the plateau floor
    raised to 400 cycles (to kill the data term that (a) leaves open at ply 2 as well), read cold at plies 1-3;
    ~2.5 CPU-h. If the trees still tie their flat, choose (B) and move the architecture work to features; if they
    pull ahead late, the ply-1 screen stands with longer ply-2 confirmations. Present the six with both numbers
    either way: the ply-1 gain (45-118) and the ply-2-field gain (0 so far).

The 60-at-ply-1 rule is NOT met: the ply-1 trees' 118 / 106 at v314 formed between v226 and v314, late, with data and time; so the data term is real for the ply-1 gain, and probit-confirm-p2 (stopped at v241 by its plateau) may have stopped before its own late gain. Methodological point, agreed and important: crownings, and so the plateau stop, are judged in the league's own
T 0.1 games, which barely see a tree's ranking edge (6.28 side note: 1-6 Elo at T 0.1 against 118 cold), while the
trees keep gaining at T 0.02 after the flats' crownings stop. So the plateau rule systematically cuts TREE runs
short (probit-confirm-p2 stopped at 242 with its trees still rising), and a floor only hides the problem behind a
number chosen by hand. The right fix is to judge the crowning test at T 0.02 for every experiment (one setting,
the champion test's policy), with the games it rates still played at T 0.1 for training, since 6.3's finding
stands that cold games are bad training data; the test's games are few (the champion test's 100), so the cost
is nothing, and the crownings then measure what the cold reads measure. Until the user approves that engine
change, the floor should be set from the trees' own late behaviour (400 at ply 2 here) and every tree run's stop
treated as provisional until a cold read of its last two windows shows no slope.

6.32 amendment (11:55): the champion test has no separate games; it reads the cycle's own round-robins, which are also the training data, so a cold crowning test means a separate cold game set. Sizing: for near-decisive games the Elo SE of n games is about 348/sqrt(n), so a 20-40 Elo cold edge at 2 SE needs SE 10-20, i.e. 300 games for a per-cycle decision at SE 20, or 1,200 for SE 10. Cheapest honest form: 48 cold games a cycle per line (current@P against champion@P, T 0.02, outside the training set), pooled since the last crowning; after 25 cycles that is 1,200 games and SE 10, enough to crown on a 20-Elo cold edge, and a crowning resets the pool. Cost at ply 2 about 0.007 CPU-s a game, 0.3 CPU-s per line per cycle, 2 CPU-s a cycle for a six-line field: a rounding error against the ~40-66 CPU-s a cycle of the tournament; at ply 1 less. The 'no cost' in 6.32 was wrong; this is the cost.

For the six and the global league: keep leaf limit 4 and router limit 4 for the error DAGs (17 leaves added nothing at ply 1 and cost 25), and let growth past that wait for leaves with more to learn (S7). The proposed six are consistent with the evidence as long as the two cluster seats keep the fresh gate and the LR seat is reported with its ply-2 guard figure (-17 / 16); an LR-router error DAG would be the one seat with no cold evidence of its own (LR error routers were the evaluators in disguise of 1.3, and LR cluster routers never earned a split under the gate), so if the user wants a second LR seat the cluster tree is the one with numbers. Note on --revise: the flag MERGES a branch when its fresh-window gain over the shadow is below MINUS that many SE
(rs/src/main.rs), so --revise 2 is the lenient gate (holds splits unless 2 SE worse), --revise 0 the strict one that
merged everything at ply 2 (probit-trees-p2), and a "positive-gain gate" would be --revise -2. Also: with crownings
judged at T 0.1 and stopping by ~360 in every ply-2 field so far, the plateau rule will stop both fields AT the floor;
"final" is v600 unless crownings continue, and the cap 900 is unlikely to bind.

(b) Stage 2, reviewed. Which variable carries the ply-2 gap, in my order, with the evidence already in hand:
    1. Data per leaf and run length: the only variable with a measured ply-2 signal (the tree was still closing at
       v399). Arm: SPLIT LIMIT 1 against 4 in one probit ply-2 field, F-P x2, D-P-Ne-ctx limit 1 x2, limit 4 x2
       (the limit-4 arm must be IN the field: the field effect moves a recipe's gain by 2x, so stage 1's lines are
       not a comparator). Drop the limit-8 arm: answered at ply 1 (25 below limit 4, third level copies, 6.32
       note), and at ply 2 it only dilutes data further. Read: limit 1 (3 leaves, 3x the data per leaf) above limit
       4 at v600 by 20 says the ply-2 gap is data and growth should be slowed (then threshold 5M, arm 3, is the
       follow-up); limit 1 tied with flat as well says no partition of any coarseness gains at ply 2, which is (c)'s
       H1 and ends the hyperparameter search. Cost ~10 CPU-h. LR only if probit shows a difference.
    2. Gate (--revise 2 against non-merging): ALREADY ANSWERED at ply 2 and not worth a field: probit-confirm-p2
       had the non-merging error tree at -17 / 18 / -49 and the --revise-2 cluster tree at -3 / -9 / -65 with 9
       leaves each, and probit-trees-p2's --revise 0 (merged to the root) at -10 / -8 / 1. Every gate ties flat;
       the gate only matters once there is a gain to protect. Park it.
    3. Split threshold 1M against 5M: the same lever as 1 (data per leaf, time to split) in a slower form; at 146k
       positions a cycle a 5M tree has 3 leaves until ~cycle 100 and 5-7 at 600, so it cannot reach 9 and the
       comparison is limit 1-2 in disguise. Run only as the follow-up of arm 1 if limit 1 wins.
    4. Router model for cluster nodes (NB / TAN / LR): predicted null for NB against TAN (same partition, 88-99%
       agreement, 6.33: within 15 either sign at any ply) and a known loser for LR cluster routers (never earned a
